Index / Class 11
Chapter 1 · Step-by-step

Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry

The mole chapter, and the most scoring chapter of Class 11 physical chemistry because every question is a fixed recipe. NEET asks direct number-based Qs: moles from mass, molecules from volume, limiting reagent, percentage yield, empirical formula from percentage data. The master key is that a balanced equation speaks only in moles, so every solution follows the same three-step pipeline: convert whatever is given into moles, apply the coefficient ratio, convert back to whatever is asked. Memorize three conversion doors (mass via M, gas volume via 22.4 L at STP, particle count via NA) plus the limiting-reagent test (moles divided by coefficient, smallest wins), and no question in this chapter can surprise you.

~2 NEET Qs50 step-by-step topicsClass 11

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • C. Atomic and molecular masses: 1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
  • D. Mole concept: n = m/M = V/22.4 (STP) = N/NA
  • E. Percentage composition: % element = (mass of element / molar mass) x 100
  • F. Empirical and molecular formula: Molecular formula = n x empirical formula
  • H. Stoichiometry: % yield = (actual / theoretical) x 100
A. Matter and its natureB. Laws of chemical combinationC. Atomic and molecular massesD. Mole conceptE. Percentage compositionF. Empirical and molecular formulaG. Chemical equationsH. Stoichiometry

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

Matter

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Anything that has mass and occupies space. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Test both conditions together: air qualifies (mass plus volume) while heat, light and shadow fail one of them. Students lose marks by calling energy a form of matter. In numericals, only matter enters mass-balance equations, which is why the conservation law is stated for mass, not for everything.
Key Formulas – Matter
matter = mass + volume two tests
Sum m(reactants) = Sum m(products) conserved
density = m/V physical marker
NEET Snapshot:
  • Anything that has mass and occupies space.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.
Back to Index

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

States of matter

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Solid, liquid, gas; plasma and BEC are the other two. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Order of kinetic energy: solid < liquid < gas < plasma; shape fixed only in solids.
Key Formulas – States of matter
K.E. solid < liquid < gas energy order
fixed volume: solids + liquids holds volume
plasma = ionized gas 4th state
BEC near 0 K 5th state
NEET Snapshot:
  • Solid, liquid, gas; plasma and BEC are the other two.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

Classification of matter

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Pure substances (elements, compounds) vs mixtures. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Pure substances have fixed composition; mixtures vary and separate physically.
Key Formulas – Classification of matter
pure = fixed composition rule
mixture = variable rule
homogeneous = single phase air, brass
heterogeneous = multi phase sand-water
pure splits: element + compound tree
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pure substances (elements, compounds) vs mixtures.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

Elements

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Pure basic form; 118 known, 94 natural. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Cannot be broken down chemically; represented by symbols from names or Latin roots.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pure basic form; 118 known, 94 natural.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

Compounds

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fixed ratio of elements; separable only chemically. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Water is always H:O = 1:8 by mass; that fixity defines a compound.
Key Formulas – Compounds
H:O = 1:8 by mass in water fixed ratio
CO2: C:O = 3:8 example
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fixed ratio of elements; separable only chemically.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.

Chapter 1 · A. Matter and its nature

Physical and chemical properties

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11A. Matter and its natureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Physical: no new substance; chemical: new substance forms. Anything with mass and volume. NCERT classifies matter first, then builds all laws on it. The classification is not decoration: pure substances obey fixed-composition laws while mixtures do not, so identifying which one you face decides which laws apply. Read every item as element (one atom type), compound (fixed ratio, chemical split only), or mixture (variable, physical split).
Deeper. Melting is physical, burning is chemical; colour and density are physical markers.
Key Formulas – Physical and chemical properties
density = m/V physical
NEET Snapshot:
  • Physical: no new substance; chemical: new substance forms.
  • Element vs compound vs mixture is a favourite one-marker.

Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Law of conservation of mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Mass is neither created nor destroyed in a reaction. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. Lavoisier 1789, sealed-vessel experiments: precipitate or gas, the balance reads the same total. This is why every equation must be balanced before any mole math. Nuclear reactions are the stated exception, and they lie outside chemistry syllabus scope.
Key Formulas – Law of conservation of mass
Sum m(reactants) = Sum m(products) mass ledger
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mass is neither created nor destroyed in a reaction.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.
Back to Index
Index / Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry
Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Law of definite proportions

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

A compound always has the same elements in the same mass ratio. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. Proust 1799; source or sample size never changes the ratio. Isotopic samples can shift the ratio microscopically, which is the standard assertion-reason trap.
Key Formulas – Law of definite proportions
H2O always 1:8 example
NEET Snapshot:
  • A compound always has the same elements in the same mass ratio.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.
Back to Index
Index / Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry
Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Law of multiple proportions

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Two elements forming two compounds: masses of one combining with fixed mass of the other are in small whole-number ratio. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. Dalton; classic pair CO vs CO2 with oxygen 16:32 = 1:2. The method is fix one element, compare the other, reduce the fraction.
Key Formulas – Law of multiple proportions
O in CO vs CO2 = 16:32 ratio 1:2
NEET Snapshot:
  • Two elements forming two compounds: masses of one combining with fixed mass of the other are in small whole-number ratio.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.

Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Law of reciprocal proportions

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Ratios in which elements combine with a third decide their mutual ratio. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. Richter; lets chemists predict combination ratios indirectly.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ratios in which elements combine with a third decide their mutual ratio.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.

Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Gay-Lussac's law of gaseous volumes

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Gases combine in simple volume ratios at same T and P. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. 2 vol H2 + 1 vol O2 give 2 vol steam; hinted at molecules.
Key Formulas – Gay-Lussac's law of gaseous volumes
2H2 + O2 -> 2H2O volumes 2:1:2
H2 + Cl2 -> 2HCl is 1:1:2 example
NEET Snapshot:
  • Gases combine in simple volume ratios at same T and P.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.

Index / Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry
Chapter 1 · B. Laws of chemical combination

Avogadro's law

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11B. Laws of chemical combinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Equal volumes of gases have equal molecules at same T and P. Five laws that experiments forced chemists to accept; every stoichiometry Q silently uses them. Conservation of mass justifies balancing equations, definite proportions justifies fixed formulas, and the gas laws justify the 22.4 L shortcut. When a question gives two compounds of the same elements, reach for multiple proportions; when gases react, think volumes.
Deeper. Basis of the mole-volume link 22.4 L at STP.
Key Formulas – Avogadro's law
V1/n1 = V2/n2 equal V, equal n
1 mol gas = 22.4 L STP child
NEET Snapshot:
  • Equal volumes of gases have equal molecules at same T and P.
  • Law of multiple proportions: same elements, different compounds, masses in small whole-number ratio.

Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Atomic mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Mass of one atom relative to C-12 taken as 12. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. One twelfth of a C-12 atom is 1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg. Relative masses have no unit; gram atomic mass (molar mass) carries g/mol. Never confuse the two in unit-carrying answers.
Key Formulas – Atomic mass
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg unit value
C-12 = 12 u exact definition
molar mass: g/mol units
1 g = 6.022 x 10^23 u bridge
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mass of one atom relative to C-12 taken as 12.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.
Back to Index
Index / Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry
Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Relative atomic mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Average mass relative to 1/12 of C-12. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. Periodic tables print averages, not single-isotope masses: chlorine 35.5 comes from 75% Cl-35 and 25% Cl-37. Multiply each isotopic mass by its fraction and add.
Key Formulas – Relative atomic mass
Cl = 0.75x35 + 0.25x37 = 35.5 worked average
Avg = Sum fraction x mass weighted
B = 10.8 (20/80) example
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Average mass relative to 1/12 of C-12.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.

Index / Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry
Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Molecular mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Sum of atomic masses of all atoms in a molecule. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. H2SO4 = 2(1) + 32 + 4(16) = 98 u, H2O = 18 u. For hydrates include the water molecules (CuSO4.5H2O counts all five).
Key Formulas – Molecular mass
H2SO4 = 98 u add atoms
H2O = 18 u add atoms
CO2 = 44 u add atoms
NH3 = 17 u add atoms
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Sum of atomic masses of all atoms in a molecule.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.

Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Formula mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Like molecular mass but for ionic compounds. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. Ionic lattices have no molecules, so NaCl = 23 + 35.5 = 58.5 u is called formula mass. Numerically it works exactly like molecular mass in mole math.
Key Formulas – Formula mass
NaCl = 58.5 u ionic sum
CaCl2 = 111 u ionic sum
Al2(SO4)3 = 342 u big example
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Like molecular mass but for ionic compounds.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.

Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Average atomic mass

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Weighted average using natural abundance of isotopes. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. The weights are fractional abundances summing to 1. Boron (20% B-10, 80% B-11) averages 10.8, another favourite numerical.
Key Formulas – Average atomic mass
Avg = Sum fraction(i) x mass(i) weighted mean
B = 10.8 example
Cl = 35.5 example
percent/100 first convert
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Weighted average using natural abundance of isotopes.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.

Chapter 1 · C. Atomic and molecular masses

Isotopic abundance

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11C. Atomic and molecular massesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Percentage of each isotope used as weight in the average. The weighing scale of chemistry: all masses are relative to carbon-12. Because single atoms cannot be weighed, chemists defined the unified mass unit from C-12 and tabulated everything else against it. Average atomic mass then folds in natural isotopic abundance, which is why chlorine reads 35.5 instead of a whole number.
Deeper. Convert percent to fraction first (divide by 100), then multiply. Forgetting this division is the commonest 1-mark loss in the topic.
Key Formulas – Isotopic abundance
fraction = percent / 100 convert first
75% = 0.75 example
check total 100% verify
More formulas – C. Atomic and molecular masses
1 u = 1.66 x 10^-27 kg
NEET Snapshot:
  • Percentage of each isotope used as weight in the average.
  • Average atomic mass uses isotopic abundance as weights.

Chapter 1 · D. Mole concept

Mole

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11D. Mole conceptStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Amount containing as many particles as atoms in 12 g of C-12. The counting unit: 1 mole = 6.022 x 10^23 particles. The single highest-yield idea of the chapter. Think of the mole as the chemist's dozen: instead of counting particles, weigh them, because molar mass converts grams to counts. Three doors lead in and out of moles: mass (divide by M), gas volume at STP (divide by 22.4), particle count (divide by NA).
Deeper. One mole is 6.022 x 10^23 entities of whatever is specified: atoms, molecules, ions, electrons. Always attach the entity name (1 mole of O2 vs 1 mole of O differ by factor 2), because examiners exploit exactly this ambiguity.
Key Formulas – Mole
n = m/M mass route
n = V/22.4 (gas, STP) volume route
n = N/NA count route
12 g C-12 definition
More formulas – D. Mole concept
n = m/M = V/22.4 (STP) = N/NA
NEET Snapshot:
  • Amount containing as many particles as atoms in 12 g of C-12.
  • At STP 1 mole of any gas = 22.4 L. STP means 0 C and 1 bar in NCERT.

Chapter 1 · E. Percentage composition

Percentage of elements

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11E. Percentage compositionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Part of total mass each element contributes. Mass share of each element in 100 g of compound; the bridge to empirical formula. Percentages turn an unknown formula into weighable numbers: assume 100 g, and each percent becomes grams directly. The same arithmetic serves solutions (w/w) and hydrated salts.
Deeper. Compute (atoms x atomic mass / molar mass) x 100 for each element, then verify the shares add to 100. A total above 100 flags double-counted oxygen in hydrates.
Key Formulas – Percentage of elements
%E = (atoms x Ar / Mr) x 100 share formula
>100 means error flag
100 g basis assume
More formulas – E. Percentage composition
% element = (mass of element / molar mass) x 100
NEET Snapshot:
  • Part of total mass each element contributes.
  • Assume 100 g sample: percentages become grams directly.

Chapter 1 · F. Empirical and molecular formula

Empirical formula

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11F. Empirical and molecular formulaStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Simplest whole-number ratio of atoms. Empirical = simplest ratio; molecular = actual numbers. NEET gives percentage data and asks the formula. The pipeline never changes: percent to grams (assume 100 g), grams to moles (divide by Ar), moles to ratio (divide by smallest), ratio to integers (multiply to clear fractions). Then n from molar mass scales empirical to molecular.
Deeper. CH2O for both formaldehyde and glucose; the ratio alone cannot name the compound. Round only at the final step: 1.33 means x3, 1.5 means x2, 1.25 means x4.
Key Formulas – Empirical formula
1.5 -> x2 clear
1.33 -> x3 clear
1.25 -> x4 clear
More formulas – F. Empirical and molecular formula
Molecular formula = n x empirical formula
NEET Snapshot:
  • Simplest whole-number ratio of atoms.
  • n = molecular mass / empirical formula mass.
Back to Index

Actual number of each atom in a molecule. Empirical = simplest ratio; molecular = actual numbers. NEET gives percentage data and asks the formula. The pipeline never changes: percent to grams (assume 100 g), grams to moles (divide by Ar), moles to ratio (divide by smallest), ratio to integers (multiply to clear fractions). Then n from molar mass scales empirical to molecular.
Deeper. Glucose C6H12O6 vs empirical CH2O shows why molar mass is indispensable. Same empirical formula can hide many molecular identities.

glucose C6H12O6 example

Chapter 1 · G. Chemical equations

Writing chemical equations

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11G. Chemical equationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Reactants left, products right, arrow between. Shorthand of a reaction; must be balanced so mass is conserved. Balancing is applied conservation of mass: equal atoms of each kind on both sides. The coefficients you write here become the mole ratios for the entire stoichiometry section, so a wrong coefficient poisons every later number.
Deeper. Add state symbols (s, l, g, aq), plus delta for heat and precipitate/gas arrows where used. A complete equation also satisfies charge balance in ionic cases.
Key Formulas – Writing chemical equations
delta = heat symbol
NEET Snapshot:
  • Reactants left, products right, arrow between.
  • Balance atoms first, then check charge for ionic equations.

Equal atoms of each kind on both sides. Shorthand of a reaction; must be balanced so mass is conserved. Balancing is applied conservation of mass: equal atoms of each kind on both sides. Deeper. Hit-or-trial strategy: balance the most complex formula first, save H and O for last, then clear fractions by multiplying through. Polyatomic ions that survive intact can be balanced as units.

atoms LHS = atoms RHS balance test

Stoichiometric coefficients

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11G. Chemical equationsStep-by-step Topic

Numbers before formulas giving mole ratios. Shorthand of a reaction; must be balanced so mass is conserved. Balancing is applied conservation of mass: equal atoms of each kind on both sides. The coefficients you write here become the mole ratios for the entire stoichiometry section, so a wrong coefficient poisons every later number.
Deeper. In 2H2 + O2 -> 2H2O the ratio 2:1:2 is a mole ratio, a molecule ratio and (for gases) a volume ratio. Stoichiometry is just this ratio applied to measured moles.

= molecule ratio micro
= volume ratio (gases) macro

Chapter 1 · H. Stoichiometry

Stoichiometric calculations

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11H. StoichiometryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Use balanced-equation mole ratios to convert quantities. Mole-ratio arithmetic of reactions. Limiting reagent decides the product amount. The universal route is given quantity to moles, moles across via coefficients, moles to wanted quantity. When two reactant quantities are given, the limiting-reagent test (moles/coefficient, smallest wins) must precede everything, because all products grow from the limiter alone.
Deeper. Template: convert given to moles, multiply by (wanted coefficient / given coefficient), convert to wanted unit. Three multiplications, zero memorization.
Key Formulas – Stoichiometric calculations
n2 = n1 x (coeff2/coeff1) ratio step
given -> mol step 1
mol -> wanted step 3
More formulas – H. Stoichiometry
% yield = (actual / theoretical) x 100
NEET Snapshot:
  • Use balanced-equation mole ratios to convert quantities.
  • Find limiting reagent first: divide each mole by its coefficient, smallest wins.

Chapter 1 · H. Stoichiometry

Limiting reagent

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11H. StoichiometryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Reactant consumed first; caps the product. Mole-ratio arithmetic of reactions. Limiting reagent decides the product amount. The universal route is given quantity to moles, moles across via coefficients, moles to wanted quantity. When two reactant quantities are given, the limiting-reagent test (moles/coefficient, smallest wins) must precede everything, because all products grow from the limiter alone.
Deeper. Test: compute moles/coefficient for each reactant; the smallest value limits. With 2 mol H2 and 0.5 mol O2 for 2H2 + O2, O2 (0.5/1 < 2/2) limits and yields 1 mol H2O.
Key Formulas – Limiting reagent
min(n1/c1, n2/c2) limits compare
2H2+O2 example 1 mol H2O
More formulas – H. Stoichiometry
% yield = (actual / theoretical) x 100
NEET Snapshot:
  • Reactant consumed first; caps the product.
  • Find limiting reagent first: divide each mole by its coefficient, smallest wins.

Chapter 1 · H. Stoichiometry

Excess reagent

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11H. StoichiometryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Reactant left over after the other finishes. Mole-ratio arithmetic of reactions. Limiting reagent decides the product amount. The universal route is given quantity to moles, moles across via coefficients, moles to wanted quantity. When two reactant quantities are given, the limiting-reagent test (moles/coefficient, smallest wins) must precede everything, because all products grow from the limiter alone.
Deeper. Leftover = initial moles minus consumed moles, where consumed comes from the limiter via ratio. Questions asking leftover test whether you tracked both reactants.
Key Formulas – Excess reagent
leftover = initial - consumed subtract
More formulas – H. Stoichiometry
% yield = (actual / theoretical) x 100
NEET Snapshot:
  • Reactant left over after the other finishes.
  • Find limiting reagent first: divide each mole by its coefficient, smallest wins.

Chapter 1 · H. Stoichiometry

Percentage yield

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11H. StoichiometryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Actual over theoretical times 100. Mole-ratio arithmetic of reactions. Limiting reagent decides the product amount. The universal route is given quantity to moles, moles across via coefficients, moles to wanted quantity. When two reactant quantities are given, the limiting-reagent test (moles/coefficient, smallest wins) must precede everything, because all products grow from the limiter alone.
Deeper. Losses come from side reactions, incomplete conversion and handling. Yield above 100% always signals impure product or arithmetic error.
Key Formulas – Percentage yield
% = (actual/theoretical) x 100 efficiency
>100% = error flag
theoretical = denominator base
More formulas – H. Stoichiometry
% yield = (actual / theoretical) x 100
NEET Snapshot:
  • Actual over theoretical times 100.
  • Find limiting reagent first: divide each mole by its coefficient, smallest wins.

Deeper. Computed purely on paper assuming complete conversion with zero loss. Every yield question needs it as the denominator.

100% conversion assume

Actual yield

Some Basic Concepts of ChemistryClass 11H. StoichiometryStep-by-step Topic

When two reactant quantities are given, the limiting-reagent test (moles/coefficient, smallest wins) must precede everything, because all products grow from the limiter alone.
Deeper. Always below theoretical in real labs; the gap measures process efficiency.

gap = loss meaning
< theoretical always
More formulas – H. Stoichiometry

Chapter 1 · Practice

Some Basic Concepts of Chemistry: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

Moles in 4.4 g of CO2 (molar mass 44) are:

Solution: n = 4.4/44 = 0.1 mol.
2

4 g H2 (2 mol) reacts with 16 g O2 (0.5 mol) to form water (2H2 + O2 -> 2H2O). Maximum H2O formed:

Solution: 0.5 mol O2 needs 1 mol H2 and gives 1 mol H2O; O2 limits.
3

Theoretical yield 10 g, actual yield 8 g. Percentage yield:

Solution: % yield = 8/10 x 100 = 80%.
4

A compound has 40% C, 6.7% H, 53.3% O. Its empirical formula is:

Solution: Moles ratio C:H:O = 3.33:6.7:3.33 = 1:2:1.

Index / Class 11
Chapter 2 · Step-by-step

Atomic Structure

Light, Bohr orbits and quantum numbers, ending in electronic configuration. NEET loves four question types here: matching spectral series to regions, rejecting invalid quantum-number sets, applying the Bohr energy and radius formulas to hydrogen-like ions, and writing configurations with the Cr/Cu exceptions. Read every spectrum as electron jumps between fixed levels: downward jumps emit photons, upward jumps absorb them, and the gap size fixes the photon energy. Quantum numbers then replace circular orbits with three-dimensional orbitals, and three filling rules (Aufbau, Pauli, Hund) place every electron. Master the Rydberg series table, the n+l order and the two anomalous configurations, and this chapter is fully secured.

~2 NEET Qs60 step-by-step topicsClass 11

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • A. Electromagnetic radiation: c = v x lambda ; E = h x v
  • B. Photoelectric effect: K.E. = h(v - v0)
  • C. Hydrogen spectrum: 1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2)
  • D. Bohr model: En = -13.6 x Z^2/n^2 eV
  • E. Dual nature of matter: lambda = h/mv
  • F. Heisenberg uncertainty principle: delta x . delta p >= h/4pi
A. Electromagnetic radiationB. Photoelectric effectC. Hydrogen spectrumD. Bohr modelE. Dual nature of matterF. Heisenberg uncertainty principleG. Quantum mechanical modelH. Quantum numbersI. Atomic orbitalsJ. Electronic configuration

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Nature of electromagnetic radiation

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Oscillating electric and magnetic fields travelling at light speed. Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. Electric and magnetic components oscillate perpendicular to each other and to travel direction, needing no material medium, which is why sunlight crosses empty space. Speed is fixed at 3 x 10^8 m/s in vacuum for every colour; only wavelength and frequency differ. The wave picture explains interference and diffraction, while phenomena ahead (photoelectric effect) force the particle picture.
Key Formulas – Nature of electromagnetic radiation
c = 3.0 x 10^8 m/s vacuum speed
More formulas – A. Electromagnetic radiation
c = v x lambda ; E = h x v
NEET Snapshot:
  • Oscillating electric and magnetic fields travelling at light speed.
  • Order: gamma < X < UV < visible < IR < micro < radio.

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Electromagnetic spectrum

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Family of radiations ordered by wavelength. Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. From high-energy gamma rays through X-rays, UV, visible (380-750 nm), IR, microwaves to radio waves, energy falls continuously left to right. Examiners ask the order directly or place a newcomer (e.g. microwaves between IR and radio). Remember: shorter wavelength always means higher frequency and higher energy.
Key Formulas – Electromagnetic spectrum
gamma < X < UV < Vis < IR < micro < radio energy falls left to right
visible: 380-750 nm band
shorter lambda = higher v link
shorter lambda = higher E link
More formulas – A. Electromagnetic radiation
c = v x lambda ; E = h x v
NEET Snapshot:
  • Family of radiations ordered by wavelength.
  • Order: gamma < X < UV < visible < IR < micro < radio.

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Wavelength

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Distance between two crests, lambda. Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. Measured in nanometres for visible light but metres inside formulas, so convert (1 nm = 10^-9 m) before computing. Red light near 750 nm has the longest visible wavelength; violet near 380 nm the shortest.
Key Formulas – Wavelength
lambda(m) = c/v from speed
1 nm = 10^-9 m convert
red ~750 nm longest
violet ~380 nm shortest

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Frequency

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Waves per second, nu (Hz). Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. Higher frequency means higher energy per photon, which is why UV burns skin while IR only warms it. Frequency is set by the source and never changes when light enters a new medium; wavelength and speed adjust instead.
Key Formulas – Frequency
v = c/lambda inverse of lambda
Hz = per second unit
higher v = higher E link

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Energy of radiation

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

E = h x v = hc/lambda. Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. One photon carries this energy while beam intensity counts photons per second: dim UV can eject electrons where bright red cannot, proving energy per photon matters, not total power. Planck's constant h = 6.626 x 10^-34 J s anchors every calculation.
Key Formulas – Energy of radiation
E = hv per photon
E = hc/lambda from wavelength
h = 6.626 x 10^-34 J s Planck constant
intensity = photons/sec not energy
hc = 1240 eV nm shortcut
More formulas – A. Electromagnetic radiation
c = v x lambda ; E = h x v
NEET Snapshot:
  • E = h x v = hc/lambda.
  • Order: gamma < X < UV < visible < IR < micro < radio.

Chapter 2 · A. Electromagnetic radiation

Planck's quantum theory

Atomic StructureClass 11A. Electromagnetic radiationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Energy comes in packets, quanta of h x v. Light as waves carrying energy; E rises as wavelength falls. Classical physics treated light purely as waves, and Planck's quantum idea (energy in packets) was born when black-body radiation refused to fit wave theory. Every later formula in this chapter, from photoelectric to Bohr jumps, uses E = hv as its currency.
Deeper. Black-body radiation curves forced Planck (1900) to assume oscillators emit energy only in whole multiples of hv. This single assumption birthed quantum theory and directly feeds Einstein's photoelectric equation ahead.
Key Formulas – Planck's quantum theory
E = nhv n whole number
More formulas – A. Electromagnetic radiation
c = v x lambda ; E = h x v
NEET Snapshot:
  • Energy comes in packets, quanta of h x v.
  • Order: gamma < X < UV < visible < IR < micro < radio.

Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · B. Photoelectric effect

Photoelectric effect

Atomic StructureClass 11B. Photoelectric effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Electron ejection when light of enough frequency hits metal. Light knocks electrons out only above a threshold frequency; proves particle nature. Wave theory predicted that bright light of any colour should eventually eject electrons, but experiments showed a hard frequency cutoff. Einstein (1905) explained it with photons: one photon hits one electron, pays the work function, and the leftover becomes kinetic energy.
Deeper. Hertz observed sparks, Lenard measured electron energies, Einstein explained both with photons. Three facts define it: instant emission (no delay), threshold frequency per metal, and K.E. depending on frequency, not intensity.
More formulas – B. Photoelectric effect
K.E. = h(v - v0)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Electron ejection when light of enough frequency hits metal.
  • Below threshold frequency no emission, however intense the light.
Back to Index
Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · B. Photoelectric effect

Threshold frequency

Atomic StructureClass 11B. Photoelectric effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Minimum frequency that can eject electrons. Light knocks electrons out only above a threshold frequency; proves particle nature. Wave theory predicted that bright light of any colour should eventually eject electrons, but experiments showed a hard frequency cutoff. Einstein (1905) explained it with photons: one photon hits one electron, pays the work function, and the leftover becomes kinetic energy.
Deeper. Each metal owns one: caesium responds to visible light while zinc needs UV. Below it, even floodlight-intensity beams eject nothing, the killer fact against wave theory.
Key Formulas – Threshold frequency
v0 = W0/h metal property
More formulas – B. Photoelectric effect
K.E. = h(v - v0)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Minimum frequency that can eject electrons.
  • Below threshold frequency no emission, however intense the light.

Chapter 2 · B. Photoelectric effect

Work function

Atomic StructureClass 11B. Photoelectric effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

h x v0: minimum energy to free an electron. Light knocks electrons out only above a threshold frequency; proves particle nature. Wave theory predicted that bright light of any colour should eventually eject electrons, but experiments showed a hard frequency cutoff. Einstein (1905) explained it with photons: one photon hits one electron, pays the work function, and the leftover becomes kinetic energy.
Deeper. Think of it as the entry fee: the photon pays W0 to detach the electron, and only the change becomes motion. Measured in eV (1 eV = 1.602 x 10^-19 J) in most numericals.
Key Formulas – Work function
W0 = hv0 entry fee
1 eV = 1.602 x 10^-19 J convert
varies by metal property
leftover = motion split
More formulas – B. Photoelectric effect
K.E. = h(v - v0)
NEET Snapshot:
  • h x v0: minimum energy to free an electron.
  • Below threshold frequency no emission, however intense the light.

Chapter 2 · B. Photoelectric effect

Kinetic energy of emitted electrons

Atomic StructureClass 11B. Photoelectric effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Excess photon energy becomes electron K.E. Light knocks electrons out only above a threshold frequency; proves particle nature. Wave theory predicted that bright light of any colour should eventually eject electrons, but experiments showed a hard frequency cutoff. Einstein (1905) explained it with photons: one photon hits one electron, pays the work function, and the leftover becomes kinetic energy.
Deeper. Brighter light ejects MORE electrons (more photons) but not FASTER ones; only higher frequency raises speed. Stopping-potential questions measure this K.E. as eV0.
Key Formulas – Kinetic energy of emitted electrons
K.E. = hv - hv0 leftover
K.E. = 1/2 mv^2 motion form
K.E. = eV0 stopping
brighter = more, not faster rule
frequency = speed rule
More formulas – B. Photoelectric effect
K.E. = h(v - v0)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Excess photon energy becomes electron K.E.
  • Below threshold frequency no emission, however intense the light.

Chapter 2 · B. Photoelectric effect

Einstein's photoelectric equation

Atomic StructureClass 11B. Photoelectric effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

hv = hv0 + 1/2 mv^2. Light knocks electrons out only above a threshold frequency; proves particle nature. Wave theory predicted that bright light of any colour should eventually eject electrons, but experiments showed a hard frequency cutoff. Einstein (1905) explained it with photons: one photon hits one electron, pays the work function, and the leftover becomes kinetic energy.
Deeper. Read it as an energy ledger: photon energy in, work function spent, kinetic energy left. Plot K.E. vs frequency gives a straight line of slope h, the historical route to Planck's constant.
Key Formulas – Einstein's photoelectric equation
hv = W0 + K.E. ledger
slope = h graph
intercept = -W0 graph
threshold = x-cut graph
More formulas – B. Photoelectric effect
K.E. = h(v - v0)
NEET Snapshot:
  • hv = hv0 + 1/2 mv^2.
  • Below threshold frequency no emission, however intense the light.

Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · C. Hydrogen spectrum

Line spectrum

Atomic StructureClass 11C. Hydrogen spectrumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Only fixed wavelengths, unlike continuous rainbow. Discrete lines from electron jumps; Lyman is UV, Balmer is visible. A hot hydrogen lamp emits only fixed wavelengths because electrons fall between fixed levels. The lower level of the jump names the series, and the Rydberg equation converts level numbers to wavelength. Balmer is the only visible series, hence the most photographed.
Deeper. Heated solids glow continuously, but excited gases emit sharp lines, each element's unique fingerprint. Absorption spectra show the same lines as dark gaps when white light passes through cool gas.
More formulas – C. Hydrogen spectrum
1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Only fixed wavelengths, unlike continuous rainbow.
  • Lyman n1=1, Balmer n1=2, Paschen n1=3, Brackett n1=4, Pfund n1=5.
Back to Index

Chapter 2 · C. Hydrogen spectrum

Hydrogen spectrum

Atomic StructureClass 11C. Hydrogen spectrumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Series of lines from electron transitions. Discrete lines from electron jumps; Lyman is UV, Balmer is visible. A hot hydrogen lamp emits only fixed wavelengths because electrons fall between fixed levels. The lower level of the jump names the series, and the Rydberg equation converts level numbers to wavelength. Balmer is the only visible series, hence the most photographed.
Deeper. Every line is one electron jump: down-jumps emit (emission spectrum), up-jumps absorb (absorption spectrum). Five named series cover lower levels n1 = 1 through 5.
Key Formulas – Hydrogen spectrum
1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2) Rydberg
down = emit rule
up = absorb rule
More formulas – C. Hydrogen spectrum
1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Series of lines from electron transitions.
  • Lyman n1=1, Balmer n1=2, Paschen n1=3, Brackett n1=4, Pfund n1=5.

To n=1; ultraviolet region. Discrete lines from electron jumps; Lyman is UV, Balmer is visible. A hot hydrogen lamp emits only fixed wavelengths because electrons fall between fixed levels. Chapter 2 · C. Hydrogen spectrum

Rydberg equation

Atomic StructureClass 11C. Hydrogen spectrumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Wave number from the two levels involved. Discrete lines from electron jumps; Lyman is UV, Balmer is visible. A hot hydrogen lamp emits only fixed wavelengths because electrons fall between fixed levels. The lower level of the jump names the series, and the Rydberg equation converts level numbers to wavelength. Balmer is the only visible series, hence the most photographed.
Deeper. Take n1 as the lower level and n2 as the upper (n2 > n1); R = 1.097 x 10^7 per metre. For emission n2->n1 and absorption n1->n2 use the same magnitudes.
Key Formulas – Rydberg equation
1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2) use
R = 1.097 x 10^7 /m constant
emission = absorption size symmetry
wavenumber = 1/lambda unit
More formulas – C. Hydrogen spectrum
1/lambda = R(1/n1^2 - 1/n2^2)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Wave number from the two levels involved.
  • Lyman n1=1, Balmer n1=2, Paschen n1=3, Brackett n1=4, Pfund n1=5.
Back to Index
Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · D. Bohr model

Postulates of Bohr model

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fixed orbits; angular momentum = nh/2pi; radiation only on jumps. Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. Three rules: electrons revolve in fixed energy orbits, angular momentum is quantized as nh/2pi, and radiation is emitted or absorbed only during jumps with hv = delta E. Stationary orbits defy classical electrodynamics by decree.
Key Formulas – Postulates of Bohr model
mvr = nh/2pi quantum condition
hv = delta E jump
More formulas – D. Bohr model
En = -13.6 x Z^2/n^2 eV
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fixed orbits; angular momentum = nh/2pi; radiation only on jumps.
  • Radius grows as n^2; energy gap shrinks as n rises.
Back to Index
Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · D. Bohr model

Radius of orbit

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

r = 0.529 x n^2/Z angstrom. Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. Hydrogen's ground orbit (0.529 A) is the Bohr radius, the atomic unit of length. Higher shells balloon as n^2 while higher nuclear charge squeezes them as 1/Z: Li2+ ground orbit is three times tighter than hydrogen's.
Key Formulas – Radius of orbit
rn = 0.529 x n^2/Z A grows as n2
r2/r1 = (n2/n1)^2 x (Z1/Z2) compare
Bohr radius 0.529 A anchor
Li2+ 3x tighter example
n^2 up, 1/Z down trends
More formulas – D. Bohr model
En = -13.6 x Z^2/n^2 eV
NEET Snapshot:

Chapter 2 · D. Bohr model

Energy of electron

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

En = -R x Z^2/n^2. Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. Negative sign means bound: zero at infinite separation, deeper (more negative) near the nucleus. The 13.6 eV ladder spacing shrinks upward, converging at the ionization limit.
Key Formulas – Energy of electron
En = -13.6 Z^2/n^2 eV bound
E2/E1 = (Z2/Z1)^2 x (n1/n2)^2 compare
negative = bound sign
More formulas – D. Bohr model
En = -13.6 x Z^2/n^2 eV
NEET Snapshot:
  • En = -R x Z^2/n^2.
  • Radius grows as n^2; energy gap shrinks as n rises.

Chapter 2 · D. Bohr model

Velocity of electron

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Falls as n rises; v = 2.188 x 10^6 x Z/n m/s. Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. Ground-state hydrogen electron races at about 1% of light speed; outer electrons crawl. Higher Z pulls faster at the same n, which is why heavy hydrogen-like ions need relativistic corrections.
Key Formulas – Velocity of electron
vn = 2.188 x 10^6 Z/n m/s slows outward
~1% of c (H) scale
Z up = faster trend
n up = slower trend
v2/v1 = (Z2/Z1)(n1/n2) compare
More formulas – D. Bohr model
En = -13.6 x Z^2/n^2 eV

Chapter 2 · D. Bohr model

Energy transitions

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Photon energy = E(high) - E(low). Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. Emission jumps fall (n=3 to 2 gives Balmer red), absorption jumps climb. Frequency follows hv = delta E, and wavelength follows from c = v lambda. Sign errors vanish if you always subtract lower from higher.

hv = delta E photon

Limitations of Bohr model

Atomic StructureClass 11D. Bohr modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fails for multi-electron atoms and fine spectra. Fixed circular orbits with set energies; energy depends on 1/n^2. Bohr fused Planck's quanta with Rutherford's nucleus: electrons circle without radiating in allowed orbits, emitting only while jumping. The model nails hydrogen and hydrogen-like ions (He+, Li2+) but collapses for multi-electron atoms. Every formula carries Z^2 for energy and Z in the denominator for radius.
Deeper. It cannot explain spectra of helium onward, fine-line splitting, Zeeman splitting in magnetic fields, or relative line intensities. Quantum mechanics replaces orbits with orbitals to fix all of these.

Chapter 2 · E. Dual nature of matter

Wave-particle duality

Atomic StructureClass 11E. Dual nature of matterStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Matter and light both show wave and particle faces. Moving matter also waves; significant only for tiny particles like electrons. de Broglie argued that if light waves act like particles, moving particles should act like waves. Electron diffraction through crystals proved him right and earned the Nobel. For cricket balls the wavelength is absurdly smaller than any detector, so the effect stays atomic.
Deeper. Light diffracts yet ejects photoelectrons; electrons diffract yet strike screens as dots. The two faces never appear in one experiment (complementarity).
Key Formulas – Wave-particle duality
diffraction = wave cue
impact = particle cue
More formulas – E. Dual nature of matter
lambda = h/mv
NEET Snapshot:
  • Matter and light both show wave and particle faces.
  • de Broglie wavelength shrinks as velocity rises.
Back to Index

lambda = h/mv for a moving particle. Chapter 2 · F. Heisenberg uncertainty principle

Uncertainty in position

Atomic StructureClass 11F. Heisenberg uncertainty principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fixing position blurs momentum. Position and momentum cannot both be fixed exactly at once. Measuring an electron's position with a photon kick disturbs its momentum: precision in one blurs the other. The floor is h/4pi, enormous on atomic scales and invisible on everyday scales. This principle kills the very idea of fixed Bohr orbits.
Deeper. Short-wavelength probes locate precisely but kick hard. The product of uncertainties, not each alone, carries the limit.
Key Formulas – Uncertainty in position
short lambda = hard kick trade
atomic scale only scope
More formulas – F. Heisenberg uncertainty principle
delta x . delta p >= h/4pi
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fixing position blurs momentum.
  • Effect matters only at atomic scale, never for cricket balls.
Back to Index

Atomic StructureClass 11F. Heisenberg uncertainty principleStep-by-step Topic

Fixing momentum blurs position. Position and momentum cannot both be fixed exactly at once. Chapter 2 · F. Heisenberg uncertainty principle

Uncertainty relationship

Atomic StructureClass 11F. Heisenberg uncertainty principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Product of uncertainties has a minimum floor. Position and momentum cannot both be fixed exactly at once. Measuring an electron's position with a photon kick disturbs its momentum: precision in one blurs the other. The floor is h/4pi, enormous on atomic scales and invisible on everyday scales. This principle kills the very idea of fixed Bohr orbits.
Deeper. h/4pi (~5.3 x 10^-35 J s) sets the unavoidable blur. For a 1 kg ball the floor is ~10^-35 m, utterly unobservable.
Key Formulas – Uncertainty relationship
delta x . delta p >= h/4pi floor
h/4pi value constant
ball: 10^-35 m negligible
electron: 10^-10 m significant
More formulas – F. Heisenberg uncertainty principle
delta x . delta p >= h/4pi
NEET Snapshot:
  • Product of uncertainties has a minimum floor.
  • Effect matters only at atomic scale, never for cricket balls.

Chapter 2 · G. Quantum mechanical model

Basic idea of quantum mechanics

Atomic StructureClass 11G. Quantum mechanical modelStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Electrons described by wave functions, not tracks. Orbitals replace orbits: regions of probability, not fixed paths. Schrodinger's wave equation gives wave functions whose squares are electron-finding probabilities. Energy levels emerge naturally with quantum numbers attached, and shapes (spheres, dumbbells) fall out of the math instead of being assumed.
Deeper. Abandon trajectories: ask only where the electron is likely found. Operators extract observable energies from the wave function.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Electrons described by wave functions, not tracks.
  • Psi^2 gives probability density of finding the electron.

Chapter 2 · H. Quantum numbers

Principal quantum number

Atomic StructureClass 11H. Quantum numbersStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Shell n; size and energy. Four numbers address each electron: shell, shape, orientation, spin. They emerge as solutions of the wave equation, not as inventions. Validity questions test the ranges: l below n, m within -l to +l, s only two values. Any set breaking these ranges is impossible, the single most asked one-marker of the chapter.
Deeper. Higher n means larger cloud and higher energy; period number of the periodic table.
Key Formulas – Principal quantum number
n = 1, 2, 3... shell
= period number table
shell capacity 2n^2 count
NEET Snapshot:
  • Shell n; size and energy.
  • n=1,2,3..; l=0..n-1; m=-l..+l; s=+1/2 or -1/2.

Chapter 2 · I. Atomic orbitals

s orbital

Atomic StructureClass 11I. Atomic orbitalsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Spherical, non-directional. s is spherical, p is dumbbell, d is mostly cloverleaf. Shapes come from the angular wave function set by l. Nodes (zero-probability surfaces) follow clean counting rules that examiners love: total nodes always n-1, split into radial (n-l-1) and angular (l).
Deeper. One per shell from n=1 upward; probability peaks at the nucleus for 1s. Size grows with n.
Key Formulas – s orbital
l=0, 1 orbital sphere
from n=1 start
1s peak at nucleus plot
NEET Snapshot:
  • Spherical, non-directional.
  • Nodes: radial = n-l-1, angular = l, total = n-1.

2s has one radial node where probability is zero. s is spherical, p is dumbbell, d is mostly cloverleaf. Shapes come from the angular wave function set by l. Deeper. 1s probability peaks near the nucleus and falls smoothly; 2s shows an inner small lobe, a spherical node, then the main outer lobe. Radial nodes = n-l-1 gives 0 for 1s, 1 for 2s.

Chapter 2 · J. Electronic configuration

Aufbau principle

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fill lower energy orbitals first. Fill by n+l rule with Hund and Pauli; Cr and Cu are the famous exceptions. Energy order follows increasing n+l (ties broken by lower n): 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p. Half-filled and fully-filled subshells gain extra stability from symmetric charge distribution and maximum exchange energy, which is exactly why chromium and copper steal one 4s electron into 3d.
Deeper. German for building-up: electrons occupy the lowest available energy states. The n+l sequence with 4s below 3d is its working form.
Key Formulas – Aufbau principle
1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p fill order
n+l rule energy
4s before 3d famous
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fill lower energy orbitals first.
  • Cr is [Ar]3d5 4s1, Cu is [Ar]3d10 4s1 for extra stability.
Back to Index
Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · J. Electronic configuration

Pauli exclusion principle

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Max two electrons per orbital, opposite spins. Fill by n+l rule with Hund and Pauli; Cr and Cu are the famous exceptions. Energy order follows increasing n+l (ties broken by lower n): 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p. Half-filled and fully-filled subshells gain extra stability from symmetric charge distribution and maximum exchange energy, which is exactly why chromium and copper steal one 4s electron into 3d.
Deeper. Two electrons sharing an orbital must have opposite spins (+1/2, -1/2). This single rule structures the entire periodic table's block lengths.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Max two electrons per orbital, opposite spins.
  • Cr is [Ar]3d5 4s1, Cu is [Ar]3d10 4s1 for extra stability.
Back to Index
Index / Atomic Structure
Chapter 2 · J. Electronic configuration

Hund's rule

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Degenerate orbitals fill singly first with parallel spins. Fill by n+l rule with Hund and Pauli; Cr and Cu are the famous exceptions. Energy order follows increasing n+l (ties broken by lower n): 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p. Half-filled and fully-filled subshells gain extra stability from symmetric charge distribution and maximum exchange energy, which is exactly why chromium and copper steal one 4s electron into 3d.
Deeper. Carbon's two p-electrons sit in separate orbitals with parallel spins, minimizing repulsion. Pairing starts only after each orbital holds one.
Key Formulas – Hund's rule
C: 2p2 split example
N: 2p3 half example
NEET Snapshot:
  • Degenerate orbitals fill singly first with parallel spins.
  • Cr is [Ar]3d5 4s1, Cu is [Ar]3d10 4s1 for extra stability.

Chapter 2 · J. Electronic configuration

Filling of orbitals

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Order follows n+l rule. Fill by n+l rule with Hund and Pauli; Cr and Cu are the famous exceptions. Energy order follows increasing n+l (ties broken by lower n): 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p. Half-filled and fully-filled subshells gain extra stability from symmetric charge distribution and maximum exchange energy, which is exactly why chromium and copper steal one 4s electron into 3d.
Deeper. Compare sums: 4s (4+0=4) fills before 3d (3+2=5); ties like 3d vs 4p go to lower n. Higher-period exceptions (5s/4d) lie beyond NEET scope.
Key Formulas – Filling of orbitals
lower (n+l) first tie: lower n
4s(4) < 3d(5) demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Order follows n+l rule.
  • Cr is [Ar]3d5 4s1, Cu is [Ar]3d10 4s1 for extra stability.

Half-filled orbitals

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Chapter 2 · J. Electronic configuration

Extra stability of half-filled and completely filled orbitals

Atomic StructureClass 11J. Electronic configurationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Symmetry plus high exchange energy. Fill by n+l rule with Hund and Pauli; Cr and Cu are the famous exceptions. Energy order follows increasing n+l (ties broken by lower n): 1s 2s 2p 3s 3p 4s 3d 4p. Half-filled and fully-filled subshells gain extra stability from symmetric charge distribution and maximum exchange energy, which is exactly why chromium and copper steal one 4s electron into 3d.
Deeper. Exchange energy counts parallel-spin pairs: d5 has 10 such pairs, the maximum. This is the entire reason behind the only two NEET-tested anomalies.
Key Formulas – Extra stability of half-filled and completely filled orbitals
Cr [Ar]3d5 4s1 anomaly
Cu [Ar]3d10 4s1 anomaly
symmetry + exchange reasons
NEET Snapshot:
  • Symmetry plus high exchange energy.
  • Cr is [Ar]3d5 4s1, Cu is [Ar]3d10 4s1 for extra stability.

Chapter 2 · Practice

Atomic Structure: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

The Balmer series of hydrogen appears in which region?

Solution: Balmer transitions end at n = 2 and fall in visible light.
2

Which set of quantum numbers is NOT possible?

Solution: l must be smaller than n; n = 1 allows only l = 0.
3

Electronic configuration of copper (Z = 29) is:

Solution: Half-to-full d-subshell stability shifts one 4s electron into 3d.
4

Electron and proton move at the same velocity. Which has the shorter de Broglie wavelength?

Solution: Wavelength = h/mv; heavier proton means shorter wavelength.

Index / Class 11
Chapter 3 · Step-by-step

States of Matter: Gases and Liquids

Gas laws plus kinetic theory plus real-gas deviation. One formula-heavy chapter; keep the conditions for each law straight, because every law holds one variable constant and examiners swap which one. Nearly every numerical reduces to PV = nRT with T in kelvin, or to comparing root-mean-square speeds via root-T over root-M. Remember the two master ideas: temperature measures average kinetic energy (same for all gases), and real gases deviate at high pressure (molecular volume matters) and low temperature (attractions matter).

~1 NEET Q33 step-by-step topicsClass 11

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • B. Gaseous state: PV = nRT
  • C. Kinetic theory of gases: K.E. avg = 3RT/2 per mole
  • D. Deviation from ideal behaviour: Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
A. Intermolecular forcesB. Gaseous stateC. Kinetic theory of gasesD. Deviation from ideal behaviourE. Liquefaction of gasesF. Liquid state

Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · A. Intermolecular forces

Intermolecular forces

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11A. Intermolecular forcesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Attractions between molecules, weaker than bonds. Weak attractions between molecules that set boiling points and states. They are orders of magnitude weaker than covalent bonds yet decide whether a substance is gas, liquid or solid at room temperature. Stronger forces mean higher boiling points, higher viscosity and lower vapour pressure, a three-way trend asked as one-markers.
Deeper. Covalent bonds run hundreds of kJ/mol while intermolecular forces run single digits to tens. Hydrogen bonding tops the intermolecular ladder, then dipole-dipole, then London dispersion present in every molecule. Boiling points follow this exact order.
Key Formulas – Intermolecular forces
H-bond > dipole-dipole > dispersion strength order
covalent ~400 kJ/mol contrast
intermolecular ~5-40 kJ/mol scale
NEET Snapshot:
  • Attractions between molecules, weaker than bonds.
  • Stronger forces mean higher boiling point.
Back to Index
Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · A. Intermolecular forces

Types of intermolecular interactions

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11A. Intermolecular forcesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Dispersion, dipole-dipole, H-bonding. Weak attractions between molecules that set boiling points and states. They are orders of magnitude weaker than covalent bonds yet decide whether a substance is gas, liquid or solid at room temperature. Stronger forces mean higher boiling points, higher viscosity and lower vapour pressure, a three-way trend asked as one-markers.
Deeper. Dispersion (instantaneous dipoles) acts everywhere including noble gases; dipole-dipole needs permanent dipoles; H-bonding needs H attached to N, O or F meeting a lone pair. Classify the pair first, then rank.
Key Formulas – Types of intermolecular interactions
H-bond: H-N/O/F condition
NEET Snapshot:
  • Dispersion, dipole-dipole, H-bonding.
  • Stronger forces mean higher boiling point.

Chapter 3 · A. Intermolecular forces

Effect on physical properties

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11A. Intermolecular forcesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Decide boiling point, viscosity, solubility. Weak attractions between molecules that set boiling points and states. They are orders of magnitude weaker than covalent bonds yet decide whether a substance is gas, liquid or solid at room temperature. Stronger forces mean higher boiling points, higher viscosity and lower vapour pressure, a three-way trend asked as one-markers.
Deeper. Stronger pull means higher boiling point and viscosity but lower vapour pressure and volatility. Water's abnormally high boiling point for its mass is hydrogen bonding at work.

Chapter 3 · B. Gaseous state

Gas laws

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Pressure-volume-temperature relations. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.
Deeper. Four named laws plus Dalton cover every gas numerical. Identify which variable is constant in the question stem; that names the law instantly.
More formulas – B. Gaseous state
PV = nRT
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pressure-volume-temperature relations.
  • R = 0.0831 L bar /mol K; keep T always in kelvin.
Back to Index

Chapter 3 · B. Gaseous state

Boyle's law

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

P1V1 = P2V2 at constant T. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.
Deeper. Squeezing halves the volume when pressure doubles; the PV curve is a rectangular hyperbola. Syringe and breathing-pump questions are Boyle in disguise.
Key Formulas – Boyle's law
P1V1 = P2V2 T const
P proportional to 1/V curve
More formulas – B. Gaseous state
PV = nRT
NEET Snapshot:
  • P1V1 = P2V2 at constant T.
  • R = 0.0831 L bar /mol K; keep T always in kelvin.

Chapter 3 · B. Gaseous state

Charles' law

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

V1/T1 = V2/T2 at constant P. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.
Deeper. Volume scales with kelvin temperature: 300 K to 600 K doubles volume. Using Celsius here is the fatal error; always add 273 first.
Key Formulas – Charles' law
V1/T1 = V2/T2 P const
K = C + 273 convert
zero at -273 C absolute zero
More formulas – B. Gaseous state
PV = nRT

Chapter 3 · B. Gaseous state

Avogadro's law

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

V proportional to n at same T, P. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.
Deeper. Equal moles occupy equal volumes, so 1 mol of any gas is 22.4 L at STP. Adding gas at fixed T and P inflates volume proportionally.
Key Formulas – Avogadro's law
V1/n1 = V2/n2 same T,P
1 mol = 22.4 L STP volume
More formulas – B. Gaseous state
PV = nRT
NEET Snapshot:
  • V proportional to n at same T, P.
  • R = 0.0831 L bar /mol K; keep T always in kelvin.

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

PV = nRT. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.
Deeper. Master equation: solve for any one variable given the other three. Density form (PM = dRT) and molar-mass form (M = dRT/P) answer most toughness variants.

Gas constant

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11B. Gaseous stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

R links P, V, n, T. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Each empirical law froze one variable: Boyle froze T, Charles froze P, Gay-Lussac froze V, Avogadro froze T and P. Merging them gives the ideal equation, and Dalton adds mixture handling through partial pressures. Unit discipline (atm vs bar, L vs m3, Celsius vs kelvin) decides numerical marks.

Topic in Depth

Total P = sum of partial pressures. Five laws collapse into PV = nRT; Dalton handles mixtures. Chapter 3 · C. Kinetic theory of gases

Postulates of kinetic theory

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11C. Kinetic theory of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Point masses, random motion, elastic collisions. Gas as tiny bouncy balls; temperature is average kinetic energy. Five postulates (point masses, random motion, elastic collisions, no attraction, negligible volume) derive pressure from wall collisions and temperature from kinetic energy. The headline result, K.E. = 3RT/2 per mole for every gas, makes temperature a molecular speedometer.
Deeper. Zero molecular volume and zero attraction are the two idealizations real gases violate. Collisions with walls are perfectly elastic, so no energy leaks.
More formulas – C. Kinetic theory of gases
K.E. avg = 3RT/2 per mole
NEET Snapshot:
  • Point masses, random motion, elastic collisions.
  • u(rms) rises with root-T and falls with root-M.
Back to Index

Chapter 3 · C. Kinetic theory of gases

Molecular motion

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11C. Kinetic theory of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Continuous random straight-line motion. Gas as tiny bouncy balls; temperature is average kinetic energy. Five postulates (point masses, random motion, elastic collisions, no attraction, negligible volume) derive pressure from wall collisions and temperature from kinetic energy. The headline result, K.E. = 3RT/2 per mole for every gas, makes temperature a molecular speedometer.
Deeper. Between collisions molecules fly straight; collisions only redirect without energy loss. Brownian motion is this dance made visible.
More formulas – C. Kinetic theory of gases
K.E. avg = 3RT/2 per mole

  • Continuous random straight-line motion.
  • u(rms) rises with root-T and falls with root-M.

Chapter 3 · C. Kinetic theory of gases

Average kinetic energy

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11C. Kinetic theory of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

3RT/2 per mole. Gas as tiny bouncy balls; temperature is average kinetic energy. Five postulates (point masses, random motion, elastic collisions, no attraction, negligible volume) derive pressure from wall collisions and temperature from kinetic energy. The headline result, K.E. = 3RT/2 per mole for every gas, makes temperature a molecular speedometer.
Deeper. Same number for H2 and CO2 at the same T: temperature erases identity. Per molecule it is 3kT/2 with Boltzmann's k.
Key Formulas – Average kinetic energy
K.E. = 3RT/2 per mol same T same energy
= 3kT/2 per molecule per molecule
k = R/NA Boltzmann
300 K: 3.74 kJ/mol value
More formulas – C. Kinetic theory of gases
K.E. avg = 3RT/2 per mole

  • 3RT/2 per mole.

Chapter 3 · C. Kinetic theory of gases

Root mean square velocity

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11C. Kinetic theory of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

u = root(3RT/M). Gas as tiny bouncy balls; temperature is average kinetic energy. Five postulates (point masses, random motion, elastic collisions, no attraction, negligible volume) derive pressure from wall collisions and temperature from kinetic energy. The headline result, K.E. = 3RT/2 per mole for every gas, makes temperature a molecular speedometer.
Deeper. Use M in kg/mol with R = 8.314; grams give thousand-fold errors. Three speeds rank most-probable < average < rms, all scaling root(T/M).
Key Formulas – Root mean square velocity
urms = root(3RT/M) compare
u av = root(8RT/piM) average
u mp = root(2RT/M) most probable
M in kg/mol units
mp < av < rms rank
More formulas – C. Kinetic theory of gases
K.E. avg = 3RT/2 per mole
NEET Snapshot:
  • u = root(3RT/M).
  • u(rms) rises with root-T and falls with root-M.

Chapter 3 · C. Kinetic theory of gases

Relationship between temperature and molecular velocity

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11C. Kinetic theory of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Velocity proportional to root-T. Gas as tiny bouncy balls; temperature is average kinetic energy. Five postulates (point masses, random motion, elastic collisions, no attraction, negligible volume) derive pressure from wall collisions and temperature from kinetic energy. The headline result, K.E. = 3RT/2 per mole for every gas, makes temperature a molecular speedometer.
Deeper. Four times the kelvin temperature doubles speed; sixteen times quadruples it. Against molar mass the relation inverts: lighter gases run faster.
Key Formulas – Relationship between temperature and molecular velocity
u2/u1 = root(T2/T1) root-T
u2/u1 = root(M1/M2) root-M inverse
4x T = 2x speed demo
Graham: rate prop 1/root M effusion
More formulas – C. Kinetic theory of gases

Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Ideal gas

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Obeys PV = nRT at all conditions. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. A hypothetical limit: point masses with zero attraction. Real gases approach it at high T and low P.
Key Formulas – Ideal gas
Z = 1 always benchmark
PV = nRT all states obey
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Obeys PV = nRT at all conditions.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.
Back to Index
Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Real gas

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Deviates when molecules attract or occupy volume. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. Every actual gas is real; ideality is an approximation quality, not a gas type. Deviation grows with liquefiability.
Key Formulas – Real gas
ideality = quality idea
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Deviates when molecules attract or occupy volume.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.
