Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Causes of deviation

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Finite volume plus intermolecular attraction. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. High pressure exposes molecular volume (positive deviation); low temperature exposes attraction (negative deviation). van der Waals constants a (attraction) and b (volume) quantify each.
Key Formulas – Causes of deviation
a = attraction vdW
b = volume vdW
(P + an^2/V^2)(V - nb) = nRT vdW eq
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Finite volume plus intermolecular attraction.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.
Back to Index
Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Compressibility factor

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Z = 1 ideal; below or above 1 real. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. One number scores ideality: measured PV over ideal nRT. Z charts vs P at various T are direct question material.
Key Formulas – Compressibility factor
Z = PV/nRT gauge
Z = 1 ideal mark
Z < 1 attraction read
Z > 1 volume read
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Z = 1 ideal; below or above 1 real.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.

Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Z vs pressure

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Dips below 1 then rises above 1 with pressure. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. The dip comes from attraction winning at moderate P; the rise comes from excluded volume winning at high P. Higher isotherms flatten toward Z = 1.
Key Formulas – Z vs pressure
hotter = flatter trend
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Dips below 1 then rises above 1 with pressure.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.

Chapter 3 · D. Deviation from ideal behaviour

Effect of temperature

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11D. Deviation from ideal behaviourStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

High T pushes gas toward ideal. Real gases deviate at high P and low T; Z measures the gap. High pressure makes molecular volume significant (Z rises above 1); low temperature lets attractions dominate (Z dips below 1). Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2, SO2) deviate most because their attractions are strongest.
Deeper. Fast molecules ignore attractions and spend little time close together. Boyle temperature is where a real gas mimics ideality over a range.
Key Formulas – Effect of temperature
high T + low P -> ideal conditions
More formulas – D. Deviation from ideal behaviour
Z = PV/nRT (1 for ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • High T pushes gas toward ideal.
  • Easily liquefiable gases (NH3, CO2) deviate most.
Back to Index
Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · E. Liquefaction of gases

Intermolecular attraction

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11E. Liquefaction of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Pull between molecules that lets gas condense on cooling. Cool below critical temperature while squeezing and gas turns liquid. Andrews isotherms reveal the flat coexistence region ending at the critical point. Above Tc, molecules are too energetic for any pressure to bind them; below Tc, compression forces condensation.
Deeper. Stronger attraction means higher critical temperature: water (647 K) vs helium (5.2 K). Easily liquefiable gases deviate most for the same reason.
Key Formulas – Intermolecular attraction
H2O 647 K high
He 5.2 K low
CO2 304 K textbook
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pull between molecules that lets gas condense on cooling.
  • Above Tc no liquefaction however high the pressure.
Back to Index
Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · E. Liquefaction of gases

Critical temperature

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11E. Liquefaction of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Above it, gas cannot liquefy. Cool below critical temperature while squeezing and gas turns liquid. Andrews isotherms reveal the flat coexistence region ending at the critical point. Above Tc, molecules are too energetic for any pressure to bind them; below Tc, compression forces condensation.
Deeper. Tc ends the liquid-gas coexistence line; beyond it lies supercritical fluid. CO2's 304 K makes it the textbook example.
Key Formulas – Critical temperature
T > Tc: never liquid rule
CO2 304 K example
O2 154 K example
NEET Snapshot:
  • Above it, gas cannot liquefy.
  • Above Tc no liquefaction however high the pressure.

Pressure needed at critical temperature. Cool below critical temperature while squeezing and gas turns liquid. Andrews isotherms reveal the flat coexistence region ending at the critical point. Deeper. The squeeze required exactly at Tc to reach the critical point. Higher Tc gases generally need higher Pc.

Chapter 3 · E. Liquefaction of gases

Critical volume

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11E. Liquefaction of gasesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Volume at the critical point. Cool below critical temperature while squeezing and gas turns liquid. Andrews isotherms reveal the flat coexistence region ending at the critical point. Above Tc, molecules are too energetic for any pressure to bind them; below Tc, compression forces condensation.
Deeper. One mole occupies Vc there; density equals the average of liquid and gas densities (Cailletet-Mathias rule context).

Index / States of Matter: Gases and Liquids
Chapter 3 · F. Liquid state

Vapour pressure

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11F. Liquid stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Pressure of vapour in equilibrium with liquid. Vapour pressure, surface tension, viscosity all fall or shift with temperature. Liquids sit between gases and solids: molecules touch yet flow. Heating raises vapour pressure (more escapees) while lowering surface tension and viscosity (looser grip), one trend line answering all three one-markers.
Deeper. Higher for volatile, weakly-held liquids like ether; lower for water. Boiling begins when vapour pressure equals external pressure, which is why water boils below 100 C on mountains.
Key Formulas – Vapour pressure
volatile = high VP rule
boiling: VP = Pext condition
NEET Snapshot:
  • Pressure of vapour in equilibrium with liquid.
  • Vapour pressure rises with T; surface tension and viscosity fall.

States of Matter: Gases and LiquidsClass 11F. Liquid stateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Skin-like pull minimizing surface area. Vapour pressure, surface tension, viscosity all fall or shift with temperature. Liquids sit between gases and solids: molecules touch yet flow. Heating raises vapour pressure (more escapees) while lowering surface tension and viscosity (looser grip), one trend line answering all three one-markers.
Deeper. Drops fall spherical and insects walk on water because surfaces contract. Surfactants and heat both lower it.

dynes/cm units

  • Skin-like pull minimizing surface area.
  • Vapour pressure rises with T; surface tension and viscosity fall.

Viscosity

Topic in Depth

Resistance to flow between layers. Vapour pressure, surface tension, viscosity all fall or shift with temperature. Liquids sit between gases and solids: molecules touch yet flow. Heating raises vapour pressure (more escapees) while lowering surface tension and viscosity (looser grip), one trend line answering all three one-markers.
Deeper. Honey high, water low; heating thins both by loosening intermolecular grip. Lubricant grades are viscosity grades.

eta = F/(A x dv/dx) definition

Chapter 3 · Practice

States of Matter: Gases and Liquids: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

A gas at 2 atm occupies 3 L. At constant temperature, volume at 1 atm is:

Solution: Boyle: P1V1 = P2V2, so V2 = 2x3/1 = 6 L.
2

At the same temperature, which gas has the highest rms velocity?

Solution: u(rms) = root(3RT/M); lightest H2 is fastest.
3

Compressibility factor Z below 1 means:

Solution: Attraction pulls Z below the ideal value 1.
4

Dalton's law of partial pressures states:

Solution: Each gas contributes its share independently.

Index / Class 11
Chapter 4 · Step-by-step

Thermodynamics

Sign conventions plus Hess plus Gibbs. Fix forever: work done BY the system is negative in the chemistry convention NCERT uses (physics uses the opposite). Most NEET Qs test three things: the delta H vs delta U bridge via gaseous moles, Hess-law algebra (flip sign on reversing, scale on multiplying), and spontaneity from delta G = delta H - T delta S signs. State functions (U, H, S, G) ignore path while heat and work remember it, which is why Hess works at all.

~2 NEET Qs48 step-by-step topicsClass 11

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • D. First law of thermodynamics: delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
  • E. Enthalpy: delta H = delta U + delta ng RT
  • F. Thermochemical calculations: delta rH = sum formation(products) - sum formation(reactants)
  • G. Second law: delta S = q(rev)/T
  • H. Gibbs energy: delta G = delta H - T x delta S ; delta G = -nFEcell
A. Basic conceptsB. PropertiesC. ProcessesD. First law of thermodynamicsE. EnthalpyF. Thermochemical calculationsG. Second lawH. Gibbs energy

Chapter 4 · A. Basic concepts

System

ThermodynamicsClass 11A. Basic conceptsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

The part under study. Universe = system + surroundings; walls decide the system type. Everything thermodynamic starts by drawing this boundary: open walls pass matter and energy, closed walls pass energy only, isolated walls pass nothing. The system type decides which quantities stay constant and which laws apply.
Deeper. Usually the reaction mixture in the flask. Defining it precisely matters because heat, work and internal energy are all booked relative to it.
Key Formulas – System
homogeneous/hetero types
NEET Snapshot:
  • The part under study.
  • Isolated exchanges neither matter nor energy.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · A. Basic concepts

Surroundings

ThermodynamicsClass 11A. Basic conceptsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Everything else exchanging with the system. Universe = system + surroundings; walls decide the system type. Everything thermodynamic starts by drawing this boundary: open walls pass matter and energy, closed walls pass energy only, isolated walls pass nothing. The system type decides which quantities stay constant and which laws apply.
Deeper. Effectively the rest of the universe, though only the nearby part interacts. Heat lost by the system is gained by the surroundings.
Key Formulas – Surroundings
universe = system + surroundings split
heat lost = heat gained ledger
q(sys) = -q(surr) sign
NEET Snapshot:
  • Everything else exchanging with the system.
  • Isolated exchanges neither matter nor energy.

Deeper. Real (flask glass) or imaginary (a region of the atmosphere). Its permeability sets the system type.

Everything thermodynamic starts by drawing this boundary: open walls pass matter and energy, closed walls pass energy only, isolated walls pass nothing. Chapter 4 · B. Properties

Extensive properties

ThermodynamicsClass 11B. PropertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Depend on amount: mass, volume, U, H. State functions ignore path; path functions remember it. Internal energy, enthalpy, entropy and Gibbs energy depend only on the current state, so their changes equal final minus initial however you travel. Heat and work are path-bound: the same start and end can involve different amounts of each.
Deeper. Doubling the matter doubles them; they scale. Molar versions (per mole) convert extensive into intensive.
Key Formulas – Extensive properties
scale with mass extensive
NEET Snapshot:
  • Depend on amount: mass, volume, U, H.
  • U, H, S, G are state functions; q and w are path functions.
Back to Index

Independent of amount: T, P, density. State functions ignore path; path functions remember it. Internal energy, enthalpy, entropy and Gibbs energy depend only on the current state, so their changes equal final minus initial however you travel. Heat and work are path-bound: the same start and end can involve different amounts of each.
Deeper. Same in a drop or a drum of the same substance. Ratios of two extensives (density = mass/volume) are intensive.
Key Formulas – Intensive properties
drop = drum demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Independent of amount: T, P, density.
  • U, H, S, G are state functions; q and w are path functions.

Chapter 4 · B. Properties

State functions

ThermodynamicsClass 11B. PropertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fixed by current state only. State functions ignore path; path functions remember it. Internal energy, enthalpy, entropy and Gibbs energy depend only on the current state, so their changes equal final minus initial however you travel. Heat and work are path-bound: the same start and end can involve different amounts of each.
Deeper. Change = final minus initial, path irrelevant. This property is what makes Hess's law legal.
Key Formulas – State functions
delta = final - initial path-free
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fixed by current state only.
  • U, H, S, G are state functions; q and w are path functions.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · B. Properties

Path functions

ThermodynamicsClass 11B. PropertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Depend on the route taken. State functions ignore path; path functions remember it. Internal energy, enthalpy, entropy and Gibbs energy depend only on the current state, so their changes equal final minus initial however you travel. Heat and work are path-bound: the same start and end can involve different amounts of each.
Deeper. Heat and work for the same change differ between a fast and a slow route. Differentials dq and dw are inexact, unlike dU.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Depend on the route taken.
  • U, H, S, G are state functions; q and w are path functions.

Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Isothermal process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Constant temperature. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. Heat flows to hold T steady while work is done; for an ideal gas delta U = 0 so q = -w. Slow Boyle-type changes approximate it.
Key Formulas – Isothermal process
delta T = 0 fixed T
delta U = 0 (ideal gas) no internal change
q = -w balance
NEET Snapshot:
  • Constant temperature.
  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Adiabatic process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

No heat exchange. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. Insulated or very fast changes: expanding gas cools because work comes from internal energy. q = 0 always.
Key Formulas – Adiabatic process
q = 0 insulated
fast = adiabatic rule
PV^gamma const ideal gas
NEET Snapshot:
  • No heat exchange.
  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.

Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Isobaric process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Constant pressure. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. Open-vessel heating against the atmosphere; work is simply -P times delta V. Most bench chemistry is isobaric.
Key Formulas – Isobaric process
w = -P x delta V push work
qp = delta H heat

  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Constant volume. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. Bomb calorimeter condition: no expansion means no work, so all heat becomes internal energy (qv = delta U).

Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Reversible process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Infinitely slow, maximum work. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. An ideal limit through continuous equilibrium states; work integrates to -2.303 nRT log(V2/V1) for isothermal ideal gas. Never fully real.
Key Formulas – Reversible process
w(rev) = -2.303 nRT log(V2/V1) max work
NEET Snapshot:
  • Infinitely slow, maximum work.
  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.

Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Irreversible process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fast, real-world, less work. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. Expansion against constant external pressure gives w = -Pext delta V. All natural processes are irreversible.
Key Formulas – Irreversible process
w(irr) = -Pext x delta V against constant P
natural = all scope
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fast, real-world, less work.
  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.

Chapter 4 · C. Processes

Cyclic process

ThermodynamicsClass 11C. ProcessesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Returns to start; delta U = 0. Name the held-constant variable and the process is named. Isothermal fixes T, adiabatic blocks q, isobaric fixes P, isochoric fixes V. Each constraint zeroes one term: isothermal zeroes delta U (ideal gas), adiabatic zeroes q, isochoric zeroes w. Reversible (infinitely slow) gives maximum work; real irreversible gives less.
Deeper. State functions net zero over a loop, so net heat equals negative net work. Heat engines live on this identity.
Key Formulas – Cyclic process
delta U = 0 round trip
q = -w balance
P-V area = work graph
NEET Snapshot:
  • Returns to start; delta U = 0.
  • Adiabatic q=0; isothermal delta T=0; isochoric w=0; isobaric P constant.

Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

Heat

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Energy flow from temperature difference. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Flows hot to cold spontaneously; q is positive when absorbed by the system. Path function, not a state.
Key Formulas – Heat
q > 0 absorbed sign
q < 0 released sign
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • Energy flow from temperature difference.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

Work

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Energy flow from organized motion. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Expansion pushes the piston outward at the cost of internal energy. Electrical and shaft work generalize the idea.
Key Formulas – Work
w = -Pext x delta V expansion
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • Energy flow from organized motion.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

Internal energy

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Stored microscopic energy U. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Kinetic plus potential energy of all molecules; for an ideal gas it depends on temperature only. Absolute U is unknowable, but delta U is measurable.
Key Formulas – Internal energy
kinetic + potential parts
delta measurable use
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • Stored microscopic energy U.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.

Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

First law

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

delta U = q + w. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Energy cannot be created or destroyed, only moved between heat, work and storage. Mayer, Joule and Helmholtz established it.
Key Formulas – First law
delta U = q + w ledger
isolated: delta U = 0 case
cyclic: q = -w case
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • delta U = q + w.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.

Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

Sign convention

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Heat to system +, work by system -. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Chemistry convention is opposite to physics for work, the number-one sign trap. Work done ON the system is positive in chemistry.
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • Heat to system +, work by system -.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · D. First law of thermodynamics

Pressure-volume work

ThermodynamicsClass 11D. First law of thermodynamicsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

w = -P(external) x delta V. Energy conserved: heat in splits into internal rise plus work out. The chemistry sign convention (heat TO system positive, work BY system negative) must be automatic before numericals. Pressure-volume work is -Pext times delta V, vanishing at constant volume.
Deeper. Free expansion into vacuum (Pext = 0) does zero work. At constant volume delta V = 0 kills work entirely.
Key Formulas – Pressure-volume work
w = -Pext x delta V formula
More formulas – D. First law of thermodynamics
delta U = q + w ; w = -Pext x delta V
NEET Snapshot:
  • w = -P(external) x delta V.
  • Chemistry sign: work done BY system is negative.

Chapter 4 · E. Enthalpy

Enthalpy

ThermodynamicsClass 11E. EnthalpyStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

H = U + PV. Heat at constant pressure; reactions are compared via delta H. Defining H = U + PV makes open-vessel heat exactly equal delta H. The delta H vs delta U bridge (delta ng RT) is pure exam gold: equal whenever gaseous moles balance.
Deeper. A state function convenient at constant pressure, where most chemistry happens. Like U, only changes are measurable.
Key Formulas – Enthalpy
H = U + PV definition
More formulas – E. Enthalpy
delta H = delta U + delta ng RT
NEET Snapshot:
  • H = U + PV.
  • delta ng counts gaseous moles: products minus reactants.

Relationship between delta H and delta U

ThermodynamicsClass 11E. EnthalpyStep-by-step Topic

Chapter 4 · F. Thermochemical calculations

Hess's law

ThermodynamicsClass 11F. Thermochemical calculationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Total enthalpy fixed by start and end states. Hess lets you add equations like algebra to find unknown enthalpies. Because H is a state function, any route between the same endpoints sums identically: reverse an equation (flip sign), multiply it (scale value), add them (add values). Formation enthalpies then give any reaction via products-minus-reactants, and bond enthalpies via broken-minus-formed.
Deeper. Add, flip and scale given equations until they sum to the target, treating delta H values identically. Path independence of H is the legal basis.
Key Formulas – Hess's law
scale multiplies rule
More formulas – F. Thermochemical calculations
delta rH = sum formation(products) - sum formation(reactants)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Total enthalpy fixed by start and end states.
  • Reverse an equation flips the sign; multiply it scales delta H.
Back to Index

Chapter 4 · G. Second law

Spontaneous and non-spontaneous processes

ThermodynamicsClass 11G. Second lawStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Spontaneous needs no outside push. Entropy of the universe rises in spontaneous change. Systems can lose entropy only by dumping more into surroundings. Disorder ranking (gas > liquid > solid, more gas moles > fewer) predicts delta S signs without calculation.
Deeper. Ice melting at room temperature is spontaneous; water splitting is not (needs electrolysis). Spontaneous says nothing about speed: diamond to graphite is favoured yet imperceptible.
Key Formulas – Spontaneous and non-spontaneous processes
delta Suniverse > 0 test
More formulas – G. Second law
delta S = q(rev)/T
NEET Snapshot:
  • Spontaneous needs no outside push.
  • More disorder: solid to liquid to gas; more gas moles.

Index / Thermodynamics
Chapter 4 · H. Gibbs energy

Gibbs free energy

ThermodynamicsClass 11H. Gibbs energyStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

G: usable energy at constant T, P. One number combining H and S to judge spontaneity at a temperature. delta G = delta H - T delta S merges energy and disorder: negative means spontaneous, zero means equilibrium. Four sign combos cover all cases: (-,+) always spontaneous, (+,-) never, the mixed two flip with temperature. Electrochemistry reuses it as delta G = -nFE.
Deeper. H minus TS in one packet; only changes matter. Standard delta G0 uses 1 bar/1 M states.
Key Formulas – Gibbs free energy
G = H - TS definition
More formulas – H. Gibbs energy
delta G = delta H - T x delta S ; delta G = -nFEcell
NEET Snapshot:
  • G: usable energy at constant T, P.
  • Negative delta G means spontaneous; zero means equilibrium.
Back to Index
Index / Thermodynamics

Deeper. Exothermic disordering reactions run at all T; endothermic ordering ones never run unaided. Temperature flips the two mixed cases.

Deeper. Standard form links K: delta G0 = -RT ln K, so K > 1 means negative standard Gibbs energy.

Chapter 4 · Practice

Thermodynamics: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

For N2 + 3H2 -> 2NH3, the value of delta ng is:

Solution: Gaseous moles: 2 - 4 = -2.
2

A process is spontaneous when Gibbs energy change is:

Solution: Delta G below zero means the process is favoured.
3

In chemistry sign convention, work done BY the system is:

Solution: Expansion work by the system carries a negative sign.
4

Enthalpy of reaction from formation enthalpies equals:

Solution: Hess: products minus reactants.

Index / Class 11
Chapter 5 · Step-by-step

Equilibrium

Chemical Kc/Kp plus ionic pH, buffers and Ksp, the longest chapter of Class 11 physical chemistry. Split it mentally: chemical equilibrium (rates equal, K expressions, Le Chatelier shifts) and ionic equilibrium (Ka/Kb/pH arithmetic, buffers, salt hydrolysis, Ksp precipitation). Le Chatelier predicts the direction of shifts while ICE tables compute the numbers. For the ionic half, three formulas carry most marks: pH + pOH = 14, Ka x Kb = Kw, and Henderson's pH = pKa + log(salt/acid). Omit pure solids and liquids from every K and Q expression without exception.

~2 NEET Qs45 step-by-step topicsClass 11

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • B. Equilibrium constants: Kp = Kc(RT)^delta ng
  • D. Ionic equilibrium: alpha = root(Ka/c) for weak acids
  • F. Ionic equilibrium calculations: pH = -log[H+] ; Kw = Ka x Kb ; pH + pOH = 14
  • I. Buffer solutions: pH = pKa + log(salt/acid)
  • J. Solubility equilibrium: Precipitation when ionic product > Ksp
A. Chemical equilibriumB. Equilibrium constantsC. Le Chatelier's principleD. Ionic equilibriumE. Acid-base conceptsF. Ionic equilibrium calculationsG. Common ion effectH. Salt hydrolysisI. Buffer solutionsJ. Solubility equilibrium

Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · A. Chemical equilibrium

Dynamic equilibrium

EquilibriumClass 11A. Chemical equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Both directions run at equal rate. Forward and reverse rates equal; concentrations freeze but reactions continue. Equilibrium is dynamic, not dead: molecules keep reacting while macroscopic concentrations stay constant. It needs a closed vessel (open ones lose gas) and constant temperature (K depends on T). Catalysts speed arrival but never shift the position.
Deeper. Looks still yet microscopically busy: evaporation-condensation in a closed bottle is the visible model. Rate equality, not reaction stoppage, defines the state.
Key Formulas – Dynamic equilibrium
rate(f) = rate(b) balance
NEET Snapshot:
  • Both directions run at equal rate.
  • Catalyst never shifts equilibrium, only speeds it.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · A. Chemical equilibrium

Reversible reactions

EquilibriumClass 11A. Chemical equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Can go forward and back. Forward and reverse rates equal; concentrations freeze but reactions continue. Equilibrium is dynamic, not dead: molecules keep reacting while macroscopic concentrations stay constant. It needs a closed vessel (open ones lose gas) and constant temperature (K depends on T). Catalysts speed arrival but never shift the position.
Deeper. Marked with double arrows; N2O4 ⇌ 2NO2 is the brown-colour demo. Irreversible ones (combustion) never equilibrate.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Can go forward and back.
  • Catalyst never shifts equilibrium, only speeds it.

Catalysts speed arrival but never shift the position.
Deeper. Attainable from either direction with the same final state, valid only at constant T, and shifted only by concentration, pressure or temperature changes.

shifted by c/P/T factors

Chapter 5 · A. Chemical equilibrium

Law of mass action

EquilibriumClass 11A. Chemical equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Rate proportional to active masses. Forward and reverse rates equal; concentrations freeze but reactions continue. Equilibrium is dynamic, not dead: molecules keep reacting while macroscopic concentrations stay constant. It needs a closed vessel (open ones lose gas) and constant temperature (K depends on T). Catalysts speed arrival but never shift the position.
Deeper. Guldberg and Waage: forward rate = kf[A]^a[B]^b, and equating both directions yields the Kc expression. Active mass means molarity for solutions, partial pressure for gases.
Key Formulas – Law of mass action
rate proportional to [A]^a[B]^b power law
kf/kb = Kc birth
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rate proportional to active masses.
  • Catalyst never shifts equilibrium, only speeds it.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · B. Equilibrium constants

Equilibrium constant

EquilibriumClass 11B. Equilibrium constantsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Ratio of products over reactants at equilibrium. Kc uses concentrations, Kp uses partial pressures, linked by RT power. Write products over reactants raised to coefficients, skipping pure solids and liquids whose active masses are constant. Kp = Kc(RT)^delta ng converts between them, collapsing to equality when gaseous moles balance.
Deeper. Large K (>>1) means product-favoured, small K means reactant-favoured. K is dimensionless in strict thermodynamics but carries implied standard states.
Key Formulas – Equilibrium constant
Kc = [P]^p/[R]^r ratio
K >> 1: products read
K << 1: reactants read
More formulas – B. Equilibrium constants
Kp = Kc(RT)^delta ng
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ratio of products over reactants at equilibrium.
  • Pure solids and liquids are omitted from K expressions.
Back to Index

EquilibriumClass 11B. Equilibrium constantsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Write products over reactants raised to coefficients, skipping pure solids and liquids whose active masses are constant. Kp = Kc(RT)^delta ng converts between them, collapsing to equality when gaseous moles balance.
Deeper. delta ng counts gaseous moles (products minus reactants). H2 + I2 ⇌ 2HI has delta ng = 0, so Kp = Kc exactly.

Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · C. Le Chatelier's principle

Effect of concentration

EquilibriumClass 11C. Le Chatelier's principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Adding reactant pushes forward. System fights the change you impose on it. Add reactant and it consumes it; squeeze and it moves to fewer gas moles; heat an exothermic reaction and it retreats. Temperature alone changes K; concentration and pressure only move the position. Catalysts change neither.
Deeper. The system consumes the added stress: more N2 pushes N2 + 3H2 toward NH3. Removing product (condensing NH3) pulls equally hard.
Key Formulas – Effect of concentration
add R -> forward shift
remove P -> forward pull
add P -> backward shift
NEET Snapshot:
  • Adding reactant pushes forward.
  • Pressure matters only when gaseous moles differ.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · C. Le Chatelier's principle

Effect of pressure

EquilibriumClass 11C. Le Chatelier's principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Favours fewer gas moles. System fights the change you impose on it. Add reactant and it consumes it; squeeze and it moves to fewer gas moles; heat an exothermic reaction and it retreats. Temperature alone changes K; concentration and pressure only move the position. Catalysts change neither.
Deeper. Squeezing N2 + 3H2 (4 mol) toward 2NH3 (2 mol) raises yield. When moles match (H2 + I2), pressure does nothing.
Key Formulas – Effect of pressure
P up -> fewer moles squeeze
N2+3H2: 4->2 demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Favours fewer gas moles.
  • Pressure matters only when gaseous moles differ.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · C. Le Chatelier's principle

Effect of volume

EquilibriumClass 11C. Le Chatelier's principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Same logic reversed. System fights the change you impose on it. Add reactant and it consumes it; squeeze and it moves to fewer gas moles; heat an exothermic reaction and it retreats. Temperature alone changes K; concentration and pressure only move the position. Catalysts change neither.
Deeper. Expanding favours more gas moles; it is pressure's mirror image. Inert gas at constant volume changes nothing.
Key Formulas – Effect of volume
V up -> more moles expand
NEET Snapshot:
  • Same logic reversed.
  • Pressure matters only when gaseous moles differ.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · C. Le Chatelier's principle

Effect of temperature

EquilibriumClass 11C. Le Chatelier's principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Exothermic favours low T, endothermic high T. System fights the change you impose on it. Add reactant and it consumes it; squeeze and it moves to fewer gas moles; heat an exothermic reaction and it retreats. Temperature alone changes K; concentration and pressure only move the position. Catalysts change neither.
Deeper. Heat acts as reactant in exothermic (cool to push forward) and product in endothermic. Only temperature resets K itself: van't Hoff quantifies it.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Exothermic favours low T, endothermic high T.
  • Pressure matters only when gaseous moles differ.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · C. Le Chatelier's principle

Effect of catalyst

EquilibriumClass 11C. Le Chatelier's principleStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

No shift, faster arrival. System fights the change you impose on it. Add reactant and it consumes it; squeeze and it moves to fewer gas moles; heat an exothermic reaction and it retreats. Temperature alone changes K; concentration and pressure only move the position. Catalysts change neither.
Deeper. Lowers forward and reverse barriers equally, so K is untouched and equilibrium comes sooner. Haber uses iron for speed, pressure for yield.
NEET Snapshot:
  • No shift, faster arrival.
  • Pressure matters only when gaseous moles differ.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · D. Ionic equilibrium

Electrolytes

EquilibriumClass 11D. Ionic equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Conduct via ions in solution. Weak electrolytes partly split; degree of ionization links Ka and concentration. Strong electrolytes ionize fully (alpha = 1) while weak ones obey Ostwald's Ka = c alpha^2, giving alpha = root(Ka/c). Dilution raises ionization, which is why weak acids strengthen (relatively) on adding water.
Deeper. Strong (NaCl, HCl) split fully; weak (CH3COOH, NH4OH) split partly. Conductivity measures the split directly.
More formulas – D. Ionic equilibrium
alpha = root(Ka/c) for weak acids
NEET Snapshot:
  • Conduct via ions in solution.
  • Ostwald dilution: dilution raises ionization.
Back to Index

Chapter 5 · D. Ionic equilibrium

EquilibriumClass 11D. Ionic equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Fraction split. Weak electrolytes partly split; degree of ionization links Ka and concentration. HA gives H+ plus A-. Weak electrolytes partly split; degree of ionization links Ka and concentration. Deeper. Weak ones set Ka/Kb equilibria; polybasic acids ionize stepwise with Ka1 >> Ka2 >> Ka3.

Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · E. Acid-base concepts

Arrhenius concept

EquilibriumClass 11E. Acid-base conceptsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Acid gives H+, base gives OH- in water. Three definitions widening the net: Arrhenius (H+/OH- in water), Bronsted-Lowry (proton donate/accept), Lewis (electron-pair accept/donate). Each later definition swallows the earlier: all Arrhenius acids are Bronsted acids, all Bronsted are Lewis, but not conversely. Conjugate pairs differ by exactly one proton.
Deeper. HCl and NaOH are textbook cases, but NH3 (no OH-) and BF3 (no H+) escape it. Limited to aqueous solutions.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Acid gives H+, base gives OH- in water.
  • Lewis widest: acid accepts electron pair, base donates it.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · E. Acid-base concepts

Bronsted-Lowry concept

EquilibriumClass 11E. Acid-base conceptsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Acid donates proton, base accepts it. Three definitions widening the net: Arrhenius (H+/OH- in water), Bronsted-Lowry (proton donate/accept), Lewis (electron-pair accept/donate). Each later definition swallows the earlier: all Arrhenius acids are Bronsted acids, all Bronsted are Lewis, but not conversely. Conjugate pairs differ by exactly one proton.
Deeper. Conjugate pairs (CH3COOH/CH3COO-) differ by one H+; water is amphiprotic. Strong acid has weak conjugate base.
Key Formulas – Bronsted-Lowry concept
acid <=> base + H+ pair gap
NEET Snapshot:
  • Acid donates proton, base accepts it.
  • Lewis widest: acid accepts electron pair, base donates it.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · E. Acid-base concepts

Lewis concept

EquilibriumClass 11E. Acid-base conceptsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Acid takes electron pair, base gives it. Three definitions widening the net: Arrhenius (H+/OH- in water), Bronsted-Lowry (proton donate/accept), Lewis (electron-pair accept/donate). Each later definition swallows the earlier: all Arrhenius acids are Bronsted acids, all Bronsted are Lewis, but not conversely. Conjugate pairs differ by exactly one proton.
Deeper. BF3 (vacant orbital) is acid, NH3 (lone pair) is base; their adduct F3B-NH3 shows the bond. Widest definition, covers aprotic systems.
Key Formulas – Lewis concept
e-pair acceptor = acid widest
NEET Snapshot:
  • Acid takes electron pair, base gives it.
  • Lewis widest: acid accepts electron pair, base donates it.
Back to Index

Chapter 5 · F. Ionic equilibrium calculations

Acid dissociation constant

EquilibriumClass 11F. Ionic equilibrium calculationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Ka measures acid strength. pH scale plus Ka/Kb arithmetic; Kw ties H+ and OH- together. The pH scale compresses ten-fold changes into single units; Kw = 10^-14 at 25 C links the two ions; Ka x Kb = Kw links conjugate strengths. Weak-acid [H+] = root(Ka x c) solves most pH numericals in one line.
Deeper. Larger Ka means stronger acid; pKa = -log Ka compresses the scale. Strong acids (Ka huge) skip equilibrium math.
Key Formulas – Acid dissociation constant
Ka = [H+][A-]/[HA] ratio
bigger = stronger read
pKa = -log Ka compress
CH3COOH 1.8x10^-5 value
More formulas – F. Ionic equilibrium calculations
pH = -log[H+] ; Kw = Ka x Kb ; pH + pOH = 14
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ka measures acid strength.
  • Weak acid pH: [H+] = root(Ka x c).
Back to Index

Chapter 5 · G. Common ion effect

Common ion effect

EquilibriumClass 11G. Common ion effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Ion in common suppresses ionization. Shared ion pushes weak electrolyte back to unionized form. Adding NaCl to saturated AgCl solution precipitates more AgCl; adding CH3COONa to acetic acid drops its ionization. Qualitative-analysis group separations are engineered on this suppression.
Deeper. Le Chatelier on ionic equilibria: extra product ion drives the reaction backward. Solubility of sparingly soluble salts falls in presence of a shared ion.
Key Formulas – Common ion effect
shared ion -> back shift logic
AgCl + NaCl demo
CH3COOH + salt demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Ion in common suppresses ionization.
  • Lowers solubility and ionization wherever the ion is common.
Back to Index

Chapter 5 · G. Common ion effect

Applications

EquilibriumClass 11G. Common ion effectStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Buffers, qualitative analysis separations. Shared ion pushes weak electrolyte back to unionized form. Adding NaCl to saturated AgCl solution precipitates more AgCl; adding CH3COONa to acetic acid drops its ionization. Qualitative-analysis group separations are engineered on this suppression.
Deeper. Group II sulphides precipitate in acidic medium (low S2-) while group IV needs basic medium. Buffer pH tuning uses the same math.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Buffers, qualitative analysis separations.
  • Lowers solubility and ionization wherever the ion is common.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium
Chapter 5 · H. Salt hydrolysis

Hydrolysis of salts

EquilibriumClass 11H. Salt hydrolysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Salt ions react with water. Ions of weak acid or base react with water and tilt pH. Salts of strong-strong pairs stay neutral; weak-acid anions hydrolyse basic, weak-base cations hydrolyse acidic, and both-weak compare Ka vs Kb. Each family owns a pH formula, and picking the right one is the whole question.
Deeper. Strong-strong salts (NaCl) skip it entirely; spectator ions never hydrolyse. Only weak-parent ions react.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Salt ions react with water.
  • Weak acid + strong base salt is basic; reverse is acidic; both weak depends on Ka vs Kb.
Back to Index

Hydrolysis constant

Kh from Kw, Ka, Kb relations. Ions of weak acid or base react with water and tilt pH. Chapter 5 · H. Salt hydrolysis

pH of salt solutions

EquilibriumClass 11H. Salt hydrolysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

From hydrolysis expressions. Ions of weak acid or base react with water and tilt pH. Salts of strong-strong pairs stay neutral; weak-acid anions hydrolyse basic, weak-base cations hydrolyse acidic, and both-weak compare Ka vs Kb. Each family owns a pH formula, and picking the right one is the whole question.
Deeper. Pick by family: acidic, basic, or both-weak (pH = 7 + 1/2(pKa - pKb), concentration-independent). Degree h = root(Kh/c) for the first two.
Key Formulas – pH of salt solutions
both weak: pH = 7 + 1/2(pKa - pKb) formula
Ka > Kb: acidic compare
Kb > Ka: basic compare
CH3COONH4 ~7 demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • From hydrolysis expressions.
  • Weak acid + strong base salt is basic; reverse is acidic; both weak depends on Ka vs Kb.

Chapter 5 · I. Buffer solutions

Acidic buffer

EquilibriumClass 11I. Buffer solutionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Weak acid plus its conjugate salt. Resist pH change: weak acid plus its salt, or weak base plus its salt. Added H+ is mopped by the reserve base (salt anion) and added OH- by the reserve acid; the salt/acid ratio barely moves, so pH barely moves. Henderson's equation quantifies it, and capacity peaks near pKa.
Deeper. CH3COOH + CH3COONa holds pH near 4.74. Added H+ converts salt to weak acid; added OH- converts acid to salt.
Key Formulas – Acidic buffer
pH = pKa + log(salt/acid) Henderson
H+ mopped by salt action
More formulas – I. Buffer solutions
pH = pKa + log(salt/acid)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Weak acid plus its conjugate salt.
  • Best buffering when pH is near pKa.
Back to Index
Index / Equilibrium

Weak base plus its conjugate salt. Resist pH change: weak acid plus its salt, or weak base plus its salt. Added H+ is mopped by the reserve base (salt anion) and added OH- by the reserve acid; the salt/acid ratio barely moves, so pH barely moves. Henderson's equation quantifies it, and capacity peaks near pKa.
Deeper. NH4OH + NH4Cl holds pOH near 4.74 (pH ~9.26). Mirror logic with OH- and salt cation.

pOH = pKb + log(salt/base) Henderson

Chapter 5 · I. Buffer solutions

Buffer action

EquilibriumClass 11I. Buffer solutionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Added H+ or OH- get mopped up. Resist pH change: weak acid plus its salt, or weak base plus its salt. Added H+ is mopped by the reserve base (salt anion) and added OH- by the reserve acid; the salt/acid ratio barely moves, so pH barely moves. Henderson's equation quantifies it, and capacity peaks near pKa.
Deeper. Reserve partners absorb small additions; large additions exhaust the reserve and break the buffer. Blood's bicarbonate system is physiology's version.
More formulas – I. Buffer solutions
pH = pKa + log(salt/acid)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Added H+ or OH- get mopped up.
  • Best buffering when pH is near pKa.

Chapter 5 · I. Buffer solutions

Henderson-type calculations

EquilibriumClass 11I. Buffer solutionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

pH from salt-to-acid ratio. Resist pH change: weak acid plus its salt, or weak base plus its salt. Added H+ is mopped by the reserve base (salt anion) and added OH- by the reserve acid; the salt/acid ratio barely moves, so pH barely moves. Henderson's equation quantifies it, and capacity peaks near pKa.
Deeper. Equal salt and acid gives pH = pKa exactly. Dilution cancels (ratio unchanged), so buffers survive watering.
Key Formulas – Henderson-type calculations
pH = pKa + log([salt]/[acid]) ratio form
equal: pH = pKa shortcut
capacity max at 1:1 peak
More formulas – I. Buffer solutions
pH = pKa + log(salt/acid)

  • pH from salt-to-acid ratio.
  • Best buffering when pH is near pKa.

Chapter 5 · J. Solubility equilibrium

Solubility

EquilibriumClass 11J. Solubility equilibriumStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Moles dissolving per litre at saturation. Ksp caps ion product; crossing it starts precipitation. For AxBy, Ksp = x^x y^y s^(x+y) links solubility s to the constant. Comparing reaction quotient Q with Ksp predicts everything: below stays clear, equal saturates, above precipitates. Common ions lower s through the same Ksp.
Deeper. s in mol/L is the Ksp-math unit; convert grams via molar mass first. Temperature dependence follows dissolution heat.
Key Formulas – Solubility
s mol/L unit
g/L via M convert
More formulas – J. Solubility equilibrium
Precipitation when ionic product > Ksp
NEET Snapshot:
  • Moles dissolving per litre at saturation.
  • Common ion lowers solubility via the same Ksp.

Ksp of the saturated solution. Ksp caps ion product; crossing it starts precipitation. For AxBy, Ksp = x^x y^y s^(x+y) links solubility s to the constant. Deeper. For AgCl: s^2; for CaF2: 4s^3; general AxBy: x^x y^y s^(x+y). Larger Ksp means more soluble for same stoichiometry.

Chapter 5 · Practice

Equilibrium: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

For N2O4 ⇌ 2NO2, the Kp-Kc relation is:

Solution: Delta ng = 2 - 1 = 1, so Kp = Kc(RT).
2

pH of 0.01 M HCl is:

Solution: Strong acid: [H+] = 0.01, pH = 2.
3

Which pair forms an acidic buffer?

Solution: Weak acid plus its conjugate salt resists pH change.
4

Precipitation begins when ionic product Q and Ksp satisfy:

Solution: Crossing Ksp starts the solid forming.

Index / Class 12
Chapter 6 · Step-by-step

Solutions

Concentration terms plus Raoult plus colligative properties, the most formula-dense Class 12 chapter. Molarity vs molality and the van't Hoff factor are the exam traps: molarity (volumetric, temperature-sensitive) for titrations, molality (mass-based, stable) for boiling and freezing work, mole fraction (unitless) for vapour pressure. Colligative properties count particles only, so electrolytes get multiplied by i. Azeotropes and deviations complete the vapour-pressure story.

~2 NEET Qs31 step-by-step topicsClass 12

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • B. Concentration terms: M = n/V(L) ; m = n/W(kg) ; x = n/total n
  • D. Vapour pressure: P = x1P1 + x2P2 (ideal)
  • F. Colligative properties: delta Tf = Kf x m ; pi = CRT ; delta Tb = Kb x m
  • G. Molar mass determination: i = observed/calculated colligative value
A. Types of solutionsB. Concentration termsC. SolubilityD. Vapour pressureE. DeviationsF. Colligative propertiesG. Molar mass determination

Solution

SolutionsClass 12A. Types of solutionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Homogeneous mixture of two or more substances. Solute dissolves in solvent; binary means two components. Nine combinations of solid, liquid and gas states cover everything from alloys to air. Homogeneous solutions show one phase; particle size below 1 nm separates them from colloids.
Deeper. Single phase throughout with particles below 1 nm that never settle. Sugar water and air look uniform at every scale.

<1 nm size

Chapter 6 · B. Concentration terms

Mass percentage

SolutionsClass 12B. Concentration termsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

(mass of component/total mass) x 100. Molarity uses litres of solution; molality uses kg of solvent; mole fraction needs no units. Solution vs solvent is the great divide: molarity and normality take solution volume (temperature-sensitive), molality takes solvent mass (stable). Dilution preserves moles (M1V1 = M2V2); temperature changes preserve molality only.
Deeper. w/w basis used for ointments and alloys. Commercial acids quote it (98% H2SO4 w/w).
Key Formulas – Mass percentage
%w/w = (w/W) x 100 mass basis
98% H2SO4 label
total = sum base
More formulas – B. Concentration terms
M = n/V(L) ; m = n/W(kg) ; x = n/total n
NEET Snapshot:
  • (mass of component/total mass) x 100.
  • Molarity changes with temperature, molality does not.

Mole fraction

SolutionsClass 12B. Concentration termsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Moles of one part over total moles. Molarity uses litres of solution; molality uses kg of solvent; mole fraction needs no units. Solution vs solvent is the great divide: molarity and normality take solution volume (temperature-sensitive), molality takes solvent mass (stable). Dilution preserves moles (M1V1 = M2V2); temperature changes preserve molality only.
Deeper. Unitless with fractions summing to 1; the only term fit for Raoult's law. Vapour and liquid mole fractions differ (y vs x).

Chapter 6 · C. Solubility

Solubility of solids in liquids

SolutionsClass 12C. SolubilityStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Rises or falls with T per dissolution heat. How much dissolves; gases follow Henry's law with pressure. Like dissolves like governs nature; temperature effects follow dissolution heat (Le Chatelier); gas solubility rises with partial pressure. Soda bottles fizz on opening because pressure release crashes CO2 solubility.
Deeper. Endothermic dissolving (KNO3) gains with heat; exothermic (NaOH) loses. Hydrated salts complicate curves with transition points.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rises or falls with T per dissolution heat.
  • Solubility of gases rises with pressure, falls with temperature.
Back to Index

Chapter 6 · C. Solubility

Topic in Depth

Proportional to partial pressure. How much dissolves; gases follow Henry's law with pressure. Like dissolves like governs nature; temperature effects follow dissolution heat (Le Chatelier); gas solubility rises with partial pressure. Soda bottles fizz on opening because pressure release crashes CO2 solubility.
Deeper. Henry's law: higher gas pressure forces more molecules in. Warm soda goes flat fast (low T favours dissolving).
Key Formulas – Solubility of gases in liquids
solubility proportional to P Henry idea

  • Proportional to partial pressure.
  • Solubility of gases rises with pressure, falls with temperature.

SolutionsClass 12C. SolubilityStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Nature, temperature, pressure. How much dissolves; gases follow Henry's law with pressure. Like dissolves like governs nature; temperature effects follow dissolution heat (Le Chatelier); gas solubility rises with partial pressure. Soda bottles fizz on opening because pressure release crashes CO2 solubility.
Deeper. Polar pairs mix, heat effects follow Le Chatelier, pressure matters for gases only. Three factors, three one-liners.

Chapter 6 · D. Vapour pressure

Vapour pressure of liquid solutions

SolutionsClass 12D. Vapour pressureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Lowered by non-volatile solute. Raoult: each component contributes its mole-fraction share. Ideal solutions obey it at all compositions with zero mixing heat or volume change; non-ideal pairs deviate by interaction strength. Total pressure is the mole-weighted sum of pure pressures.
Deeper. Fewer surface molecules escape, so boiling point rises and freezing point falls downstream. Relative lowering equals solute mole fraction.
Key Formulas – Vapour pressure of liquid solutions
delta P = x2 x P0 lowering
delta P/P0 = x2 relative
More formulas – D. Vapour pressure
P = x1P1 + x2P2 (ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Lowered by non-volatile solute.
  • Ideal solutions obey Raoult at all compositions.
Back to Index
Index / Solutions
Chapter 6 · D. Vapour pressure

Raoult's law

SolutionsClass 12D. Vapour pressureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Partial pressure = mole fraction x pure pressure. Raoult: each component contributes its mole-fraction share. Ideal solutions obey it at all compositions with zero mixing heat or volume change; non-ideal pairs deviate by interaction strength. Total pressure is the mole-weighted sum of pure pressures.
Deeper. Apply per volatile component, then sum for total: P = x1P1(0) + x2P2(0). Liquid and vapour compositions differ (y vs x).
Key Formulas – Raoult's law
p1 = x1 x P1(0) each
P = p1 + p2 total
More formulas – D. Vapour pressure
P = x1P1 + x2P2 (ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Partial pressure = mole fraction x pure pressure.
  • Ideal solutions obey Raoult at all compositions.
Back to Index

Chapter 6 · D. Vapour pressure

Ideal solutions

SolutionsClass 12D. Vapour pressureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Obey Raoult; delta H mixing is zero. Raoult: each component contributes its mole-fraction share. Ideal solutions obey it at all compositions with zero mixing heat or volume change; non-ideal pairs deviate by interaction strength. Total pressure is the mole-weighted sum of pure pressures.
Deeper. Benzene-toluene is the textbook pair: similar molecules, A-B forces match A-A and B-B. No heat, no volume change on mixing.
Key Formulas – Ideal solutions
delta H(mix) = 0 no heat
delta V(mix) = 0 no volume change
More formulas – D. Vapour pressure
P = x1P1 + x2P2 (ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Obey Raoult; delta H mixing is zero.
  • Ideal solutions obey Raoult at all compositions.
Back to Index
Index / Solutions
Chapter 6 · D. Vapour pressure

Non-ideal solutions

SolutionsClass 12D. Vapour pressureStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Deviate due to unequal interactions. Raoult: each component contributes its mole-fraction share. Ideal solutions obey it at all compositions with zero mixing heat or volume change; non-ideal pairs deviate by interaction strength. Total pressure is the mole-weighted sum of pure pressures.
Deeper. Most real pairs deviate: weaker cross-attraction pushes pressure up, stronger pulls it down. Deviations lead directly to azeotropes.
More formulas – D. Vapour pressure
P = x1P1 + x2P2 (ideal)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Deviate due to unequal interactions.
  • Ideal solutions obey Raoult at all compositions.
Back to Index

Chapter 6 · E. Deviations

Positive deviation

SolutionsClass 12E. DeviationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Higher vapour pressure than ideal. Positive deviation: weaker bonds, higher pressure; negative: stronger bonds, lower pressure. A-B interaction versus the A-A/B-B average decides the sign. Large deviations create azeotropes that boil unchanged and defeat simple distillation.
Deeper. Weak A-B pull lets molecules escape easily; ethanol-water is the classic. Minimum-boiling azeotrope results.
Key Formulas – Positive deviation
P(obs) > P(Raoult) high
delta H mix +ve sign
NEET Snapshot:
  • Higher vapour pressure than ideal.
  • Azeotropes boil unchanged: ethanol-water (minimum), HNO3-water (maximum).
Back to Index
Index / Solutions
Chapter 6 · E. Deviations

Negative deviation

SolutionsClass 12E. DeviationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Lower vapour pressure than ideal. Positive deviation: weaker bonds, higher pressure; negative: stronger bonds, lower pressure. A-B interaction versus the A-A/B-B average decides the sign. Large deviations create azeotropes that boil unchanged and defeat simple distillation.
Deeper. Strong A-B pull (H-bonding) holds molecules back; acetone-chloroform is the classic. Maximum-boiling azeotrope results.
Key Formulas – Negative deviation
P(obs) < P(Raoult) low
delta H mix -ve sign
NEET Snapshot:
  • Lower vapour pressure than ideal.
  • Azeotropes boil unchanged: ethanol-water (minimum), HNO3-water (maximum).

Chapter 6 · E. Deviations

Azeotropes

SolutionsClass 12E. DeviationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Constant-boiling mixtures. Positive deviation: weaker bonds, higher pressure; negative: stronger bonds, lower pressure. A-B interaction versus the A-A/B-B average decides the sign. Large deviations create azeotropes that boil unchanged and defeat simple distillation.
Deeper. Cannot separate by simple distillation: 95.6% ethanol-water and 68% HNO3-water are the two memorized examples.
Key Formulas – Azeotropes
95.6% ethanol famous
68% HNO3 famous
NEET Snapshot:
  • Constant-boiling mixtures.
  • Azeotropes boil unchanged: ethanol-water (minimum), HNO3-water (maximum).
Back to Index
Index / Solutions
Chapter 6 · F. Colligative properties

Relative lowering of vapour pressure

SolutionsClass 12F. Colligative propertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Equals solute mole fraction. Depend on particle count only: relative lowering, elevation, depression, osmotic pressure. Four properties, one cause (solute particles), with molality driving three and molarity driving osmosis. Multiply every result by van't Hoff i for electrolytes. Osmotic pressure's sensitivity makes it the choice for polymer molar masses.
Deeper. The only colligative using mole fraction instead of molality. Independent of solute identity.
Key Formulas – Relative lowering of vapour pressure
delta P/P0 = x2 fraction
More formulas – F. Colligative properties
delta Tf = Kf x m ; pi = CRT ; delta Tb = Kb x m
NEET Snapshot:
  • Equals solute mole fraction.
  • Osmotic pressure uses molarity and kelvin, pi = CRT.
Back to Index
Index / Solutions
Chapter 6 · F. Colligative properties

Elevation of boiling point

SolutionsClass 12F. Colligative propertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

delta Tb = Kb x m. Depend on particle count only: relative lowering, elevation, depression, osmotic pressure. Four properties, one cause (solute particles), with molality driving three and molarity driving osmosis. Multiply every result by van't Hoff i for electrolytes. Osmotic pressure's sensitivity makes it the choice for polymer molar masses.
Deeper. Non-volatile solute lifts boiling point; water's Kb = 0.52 K kg/mol. Salted cooking water boils marginally hotter.
Key Formulas – Elevation of boiling point
delta Tb = Kb x m formula
Kb water = 0.52 K kg/mol constant
More formulas – F. Colligative properties
delta Tf = Kf x m ; pi = CRT ; delta Tb = Kb x m

  • delta Tb = Kb x m.
  • Osmotic pressure uses molarity and kelvin, pi = CRT.

Chapter 6 · F. Colligative properties

Depression of freezing point

SolutionsClass 12F. Colligative propertiesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

delta Tf = Kf x m. Depend on particle count only: relative lowering, elevation, depression, osmotic pressure. Four properties, one cause (solute particles), with molality driving three and molarity driving osmosis. Multiply every result by van't Hoff i for electrolytes. Osmotic pressure's sensitivity makes it the choice for polymer molar masses.
Deeper. Salt on icy roads and antifreeze coolant both exploit it; water's Kf = 1.86 K kg/mol. Larger effect than elevation (Kf > Kb).

Chapter 6 · G. Molar mass determination

Molar mass from colligative properties

SolutionsClass 12G. Molar mass determinationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Back-calculate M from delta T or pi. Colligative values give molar mass; abnormal values reveal association or dissociation. Back-calculate M from delta T or pi; when observed M disagrees, association (joining, i < 1) or dissociation (splitting, i > 1) is at work. Acetic acid dimerises in benzene (i ~ 0.5); NaCl splits toward 2.
Deeper. M = Kf w 1000/(delta Tf x W) for freezing work; M = wRT/(pi V) for osmotic work. Match the formula to the given data.
Key Formulas – Molar mass from colligative properties
M = Kf w 1000/(delta Tf W) from freezing
M = wRT/(pi V) from osmosis
w = solute g symbols
W = solvent g symbols
More formulas – G. Molar mass determination
i = observed/calculated colligative value

  • Back-calculate M from delta T or pi.
  • NaCl gives i near 2; acetic acid in benzene dimerises, i near 0.5.

SolutionsClass 12G. Molar mass determinationStep-by-step Topic

i corrects for extra or fewer particles. Colligative values give molar mass; abnormal values reveal association or dissociation. Back-calculate M from delta T or pi; when observed M disagrees, association (joining, i < 1) or dissociation (splitting, i > 1) is at work. Deeper. Multiply every colligative result by i; degree formulas alpha = (i-1)/(n-1) for dissociation quantify it.

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

Which concentration term is independent of temperature?

Solution: Molality uses kg of solvent, unaffected by expansion.
2

NaCl in water gives van't Hoff factor i close to:

Solution: Each unit splits into Na+ and Cl-, doubling particles.
3

Ethanol + water shows:

Solution: Weaker cross-bonds raise vapour pressure above ideal.
4

Osmotic pressure of a solution is given by:

Solution: Same form as the gas equation with molarity.

Index / Class 12
Chapter 7 · Step-by-step

Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry

Oxidation numbers plus cells plus Nernst plus conductance, the most calculation-diverse Class 12 chapter. Assign oxidation numbers first in every redox question, then follow the electrons: lost at the anode, gained at the cathode, counted by Faraday, priced by Nernst. Three master formulas carry the chapter: Ecell = E(cathode) - E(anode) for spontaneity, Nernst for concentration effects, and W = E x Q/96500 for electrolysis mass. Conductance adds Kohlrausch and the dilution trends.

~2 NEET Qs47 step-by-step topicsClass 12

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • E. Electrode potential: Ecell = E(cathode) - E(anode)
  • F. Nernst equation: E = E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q at 298 K
  • G. Gibbs energy and EMF: delta G = -nFEcell
  • H. Conductance: Molar conductivity = kappa x 1000 / M
  • I. Kohlrausch's law: Molar0 = sum ion contributions
  • J. Electrolysis: W = Z x Q ; W = E x Q/96500
A. Oxidation and reductionB. Redox reactionsC. Balancing redox reactionsD. Electrochemical cellsE. Electrode potentialF. Nernst equationG. Gibbs energy and EMFH. ConductanceI. Kohlrausch's lawJ. ElectrolysisK. BatteriesL. Corrosion

Index / Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry
Chapter 7 · A. Oxidation and reduction

Oxidation

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12A. Oxidation and reductionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Loss of electrons. Oxidation loses electrons (number rises); reduction gains them. OIL-RIG plus oxidation-number tracking turns every redox puzzle into bookkeeping: assign numbers by priority rules, spot the riser (oxidized) and the faller (reduced), then name agents oppositely. Fluorine's -1 and peroxide's -1 are the favourite traps.
Deeper. Oxidation number rises in the process: Fe2+ to Fe3+ is oxidation. OIL (oxidation is loss) fixes the direction forever.
Key Formulas – Oxidation
Fe2+ -> Fe3+ demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Loss of electrons.
  • OIL-RIG: oxidation is loss, reduction is gain.
Back to Index

Fluorine's -1 and peroxide's -1 are the favourite traps.
Deeper. Oxidation number falls in the process: Cu2+ to Cu is reduction. RIG (reduction is gain) pairs with OIL as one mnemonic.

Chapter 7 · A. Oxidation and reduction

Rules for assigning oxidation number

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12A. Oxidation and reductionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

F is -1, O usually -2, alkali +1, sum equals charge. Oxidation loses electrons (number rises); reduction gains them. OIL-RIG plus oxidation-number tracking turns every redox puzzle into bookkeeping: assign numbers by priority rules, spot the riser (oxidized) and the faller (reduced), then name agents oppositely. Fluorine's -1 and peroxide's -1 are the favourite traps.
Deeper. Priority order settles conflicts: F beats O (so OF2 has O at +2), peroxides put O at -1, superoxides at -1/2, metal hydrides put H at -1. Blanket -2 for oxygen is the classic trap.
Key Formulas – Rules for assigning oxidation number
O -2 (peroxide -1, superoxide -1/2) traps
alkali +1, H +1 standards
OF2: O +2 exception
NEET Snapshot:
  • F is -1, O usually -2, alkali +1, sum equals charge.
  • OIL-RIG: oxidation is loss, reduction is gain.

Chapter 7 · B. Redox reactions

Combination reactions

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12B. Redox reactionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Two reactants join to one product. Four families: combination, decomposition, displacement, disproportionation. Combination joins, decomposition splits, displacement swaps by reactivity series, disproportionation splits one species both ways. Not every member is redox (CaO + CO2 combines with no electron change), so check oxidation numbers before classifying.
Deeper. C + O2 -> CO2 is redox but CaO + CO2 -> CaCO3 is not: joining alone proves nothing. Always verify number changes.
Key Formulas – Combination reactions
C + O2 -> CO2 redox type
CaO + CO2: non-redox contrast
A + B -> AB form
S + O2 example
NEET Snapshot:
  • Two reactants join to one product.
  • Disproportionation: same element oxidized and reduced at once.
Back to Index

Decomposition reactions

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12B. Redox reactionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

One reactant splits. Four families: combination, decomposition, displacement, disproportionation. Combination joins, decomposition splits, displacement swaps by reactivity series, disproportionation splits one species both ways. Not every member is redox (CaO + CO2 combines with no electron change), so check oxidation numbers before classifying.
Deeper. Heating KClO3 gives KCl + O2 with chlorine falling and oxygen rising. CaCO3 -> CaO + CO2, by contrast, is non-redox.

2KClO3 -> 2KCl + 3O2 redox type
AB -> A + B form

One element replaces another. Four families: combination, decomposition, displacement, disproportionation. Combination joins, decomposition splits, displacement swaps by reactivity series, disproportionation splits one species both ways. Deeper. The reactivity series predicts winners: Zn displaces Cu from CuSO4, but Cu never displaces Zn. Hydrogen displacement by metals orders the series.

Same species both oxidizes and reduces. Four families: combination, decomposition, displacement, disproportionation. Deeper. Cl2 in cold NaOH gives Cl- plus ClO-; H2O2 gives H2O plus O2. One reactant appears on both product sides in split oxidation states.

Index / Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry
Chapter 7 · C. Balancing redox reactions

Oxidation number method

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12C. Balancing redox reactionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Equalize electron change via numbers. Two methods: oxidation-number or ion-electron; both balance atoms and charge. The oxidation-number method equalizes electron rise and fall; the ion-electron method splits half-reactions and balances O with H2O, H with H+ (acidic), finishing with OH- for basic medium. Both must end atom- and charge-balanced.
Deeper. Compute total rise and total fall, cross-multiply coefficients to equalize, then balance remaining atoms by inspection. Fastest for simple equations.
Key Formulas – Oxidation number method
rise = fall electron ledger
atoms + charge verify
NEET Snapshot:
  • Equalize electron change via numbers.
  • Acidic medium: balance O with H2O, H with H+; basic adds OH- steps.
Back to Index
Index / Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry
Chapter 7 · C. Balancing redox reactions

Ion-electron method

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12C. Balancing redox reactionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Split into half-reactions, then add. Two methods: oxidation-number or ion-electron; both balance atoms and charge. The oxidation-number method equalizes electron rise and fall; the ion-electron method splits half-reactions and balances O with H2O, H with H+ (acidic), finishing with OH- for basic medium. Both must end atom- and charge-balanced.
Deeper. Balance atoms except O/H, then O via H2O, H via H+, charge via electrons, scale halves equally and add. For basic medium, add OH- to neutralize H+ at the end.
Key Formulas – Ion-electron method
acid: O via H2O, H via H+ steps
base: +OH- both sides finish
scale equal add
NEET Snapshot:
  • Split into half-reactions, then add.
  • Acidic medium: balance O with H2O, H with H+; basic adds OH- steps.

Chapter 7 · D. Electrochemical cells

Electrochemical cell

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12D. Electrochemical cellsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Converts chemical and electrical energy. Galvanic makes electricity; electrolytic consumes it; anode is oxidation in both. AN OX and RED CAT never change; only the polarity flips (galvanic anode negative, electrolytic anode positive). The Daniel cell (Zn|Zn2+||Cu2+|Cu, 1.10 V) is the working model for every cell question.
Deeper. Two electrodes dipping in electrolyte form the hardware; the salt bridge completes the inner circuit while blocking mixing.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Converts chemical and electrical energy.
  • Galvanic: anode negative, cathode positive.

Deeper. Daniel cell Zn|Zn2+||Cu2+|Cu delivers 1.10 V with zinc dissolving and copper depositing. Single vertical line is phase boundary, double is salt bridge.

Chapter 7 · E. Electrode potential

Electrode potential

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12E. Electrode potentialStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Tendency to gain or lose electrons. Hydrogen electrode at zero anchors every other potential. All reduction potentials are measured against SHE (Pt, H+, H2 at 1 M and 1 bar, fixed 0.00 V). Cell EMF is cathode minus anode using reduction values for both; positive means spontaneous. The Zn-Cu 1.10 V result falls straight out.
Deeper. Measured against hydrogen reference; higher reduction potential means stronger oxidizer. Fluorine tops the table.
Key Formulas – Electrode potential
higher = oxidizer read
More formulas – E. Electrode potential
Ecell = E(cathode) - E(anode)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Tendency to gain or lose electrons.
  • Positive Ecell means spontaneous cell.

Chapter 7 · E. Electrode potential

Cell potential

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12E. Electrode potentialStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Cathode minus anode potentials. Hydrogen electrode at zero anchors every other potential. All reduction potentials are measured against SHE (Pt, H+, H2 at 1 M and 1 bar, fixed 0.00 V). Cell EMF is cathode minus anode using reduction values for both; positive means spontaneous. The Zn-Cu 1.10 V result falls straight out.
Deeper. Use reduction values for both half-cells: Zn-Cu gives 0.34 - (-0.76) = 1.10 V. Sign errors vanish with this single formula.

EMF of cell

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12E. Electrode potentialStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Open-circuit voltage. Hydrogen electrode at zero anchors every other potential. All reduction potentials are measured against SHE (Pt, H+, H2 at 1 M and 1 bar, fixed 0.00 V). Cell EMF is cathode minus anode using reduction values for both; positive means spontaneous. The Zn-Cu 1.10 V result falls straight out.
Deeper. Read with no current drawn so concentrations stay standard. Drops once current flows (polarization).
More formulas – E. Electrode potential
Ecell = E(cathode) - E(anode)
NEET Snapshot:
  • Open-circuit voltage.
  • Positive Ecell means spontaneous cell.
Back to Index
Index / Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry
Chapter 7 · F. Nernst equation

Nernst equation

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12F. Nernst equationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

E from standard value and reaction quotient. Cell voltage falls as products build up. The general form E = E0 - (RT/nF) ln Q collapses at 298 K to E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q. At equilibrium E hits zero, giving log K = nE0/0.0591 directly. Count n (moles of electrons) and omit solids from Q.
Deeper. General form carries 2.303RT/nF; at 298 K the prefactor is 0.0591/n for log base 10. Applies to half-cells and full cells alike.
Key Formulas – Nernst equation
E = E0 - (RT/nF) ln Q general
E = E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q 298 K
half + full scope
ln vs log 2.303
More formulas – F. Nernst equation
E = E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q at 298 K
NEET Snapshot:
  • E from standard value and reaction quotient.
  • At equilibrium E = 0, so log K = nE0/0.0591.
Back to Index

Chapter 7 · F. Nernst equation

Effect of concentration

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12F. Nernst equationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Higher product ion lowers E. Cell voltage falls as products build up. The general form E = E0 - (RT/nF) ln Q collapses at 298 K to E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q. At equilibrium E hits zero, giving log K = nE0/0.0591 directly. Count n (moles of electrons) and omit solids from Q.
Deeper. Diluting the cathode side or concentrating the anode side both drop voltage. Concentration cells run purely on this difference.
Key Formulas – Effect of concentration
Q up -> E down trend
More formulas – F. Nernst equation
E = E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q at 298 K
NEET Snapshot:
  • Higher product ion lowers E.
  • At equilibrium E = 0, so log K = nE0/0.0591.

Chapter 7 · F. Nernst equation

Cell potential calculations

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12F. Nernst equationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Plug Q with correct n. Cell voltage falls as products build up. The general form E = E0 - (RT/nF) ln Q collapses at 298 K to E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q. At equilibrium E hits zero, giving log K = nE0/0.0591 directly. Count n (moles of electrons) and omit solids from Q.
Deeper. Solids and pure liquids vanish from Q; gases enter as pressures. Wrong n is the commonest Nernst error.
Key Formulas – Cell potential calculations
n = e- moles count
Q = products/reactants form
More formulas – F. Nernst equation
E = E0 - (0.0591/n) log Q at 298 K
NEET Snapshot:
  • Plug Q with correct n.
  • At equilibrium E = 0, so log K = nE0/0.0591.

Chapter 7 · G. Gibbs energy and EMF

Relationship between delta G and cell potential

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12G. Gibbs energy and EMFStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

delta G = -nFE. Electrical work and free energy are the same number in different units. delta G = -nFE ties spontaneity to voltage: positive E means negative G means spontaneous. Setting E = 0 at equilibrium converts standard EMF into the equilibrium constant.
Deeper. n counts moles of electrons transferred, F = 96500 C/mol prices them. Maximum electrical work equals -delta G.
Key Formulas – Relationship between delta G and cell potential
delta G = -nFEcell bridge
n = e- mol count
F = 96500 price
E+ means G- signs
More formulas – G. Gibbs energy and EMF
delta G = -nFEcell
NEET Snapshot:
  • delta G = -nFE.
  • Spontaneous cell: E positive, delta G negative.
