Chapter 7 · G. Gibbs energy and EMF

Equilibrium constant and EMF

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12G. Gibbs energy and EMFStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

E0 gives K via Nernst at E = 0. Electrical work and free energy are the same number in different units. delta G = -nFE ties spontaneity to voltage: positive E means negative G means spontaneous. Setting E = 0 at equilibrium converts standard EMF into the equilibrium constant.
Deeper. Bigger standard EMF means astronomically bigger K: log K = nE0/0.0591. Batteries die as E decays toward zero.
Key Formulas – Equilibrium constant and EMF
log K = nE0/0.0591 to K
E = 0 at eqm set
More formulas – G. Gibbs energy and EMF
delta G = -nFEcell

  • Spontaneous cell: E positive, delta G negative.

Chapter 7 · H. Conductance

Conductance

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12H. ConductanceStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Inverse of resistance, unit siemens. Conductivity rises with ions; molar conductivity rises on dilution for weak electrolytes. Conductance (S) inverts resistance; conductivity kappa is the material property; molar conductivity divides by molarity. Dilution lowers kappa (fewer ions per volume) but raises molar conductivity (more ionization per mole), steeply for weak electrolytes.
Deeper. G = 1/R; old unit mho survives in RC-device labels. Measured with conductivity bridges.
Key Formulas – Conductance
G = 1/R inverse
More formulas – H. Conductance
Molar conductivity = kappa x 1000 / M
NEET Snapshot:
  • Inverse of resistance, unit siemens.
  • Strong electrolytes: small rise; weak: steep rise on dilution.

Chapter 7 · H. Conductance

Variation of conductivity with concentration

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12H. ConductanceStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Falls with dilution per volume, molar rises. Conductivity rises with ions; molar conductivity rises on dilution for weak electrolytes. Conductance (S) inverts resistance; conductivity kappa is the material property; molar conductivity divides by molarity. Dilution lowers kappa (fewer ions per volume) but raises molar conductivity (more ionization per mole), steeply for weak electrolytes.

Chapter 7 · I. Kohlrausch's law

Kohlrausch law

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12I. Kohlrausch's lawStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Infinite-dilution molar values add up by ion. At infinite dilution each ion contributes independently. Molar conductivities add by ion shares, letting chemists build weak-electrolyte values from strong-electrolyte data. Degree of dissociation follows as Lambda m / Lambda0, unlocking Ka without titration.
Deeper. Lambda0 = nu+ lambda0+ + nu- lambda0-: NaCl's limit splits into Na+ and Cl- shares. Ions migrate independently when fully separated.
Key Formulas – Kohlrausch law
Lambda0 = Sum nu(i) lambda0(i) add ions
More formulas – I. Kohlrausch's law
Molar0 = sum ion contributions
NEET Snapshot:
  • Infinite-dilution molar values add up by ion.
  • Gets Ka of weak electrolytes from conductivity gap.
Back to Index

Chapter 7 · I. Kohlrausch's law

Applications

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12I. Kohlrausch's lawStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Weak electrolyte dissociation, solubility of spar classics. At infinite dilution each ion contributes independently. Molar conductivities add by ion shares, letting chemists build weak-electrolyte values from strong-electrolyte data. Degree of dissociation follows as Lambda m / Lambda0, unlocking Ka without titration.
Deeper. Finds alpha and Ka for acetic acid; finds Ksp of AgCl from near-zero conductance. Two highest-yield uses.
Key Formulas – Applications
alpha = Lambda m/Lambda0 degree
Ka = c alpha^2/(1-alpha) follow-up
More formulas – I. Kohlrausch's law
Molar0 = sum ion contributions
NEET Snapshot:
  • Weak electrolyte dissociation, solubility of spar classics.
  • Gets Ka of weak electrolytes from conductivity gap.

Index / Redox Reactions and Electrochemistry
Chapter 7 · J. Electrolysis

Faraday's laws

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12J. ElectrolysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Mass proportional to charge and equivalent mass. Faraday: product mass follows charge passed. First law (W proportional to Q) times equivalent mass gives W = E x Q/96500. Convert grams to moles of electrons via n-factor, multiply by 96500 for coulombs, divide by current for seconds. 96500 C = 1 Faraday = 1 mole of electrons anchors everything.
Deeper. First: W = ZQ for one cell; second: same charge deposits masses in equivalent-mass ratio across cells. Both merge into W = E Q/96500.
Key Formulas – Faraday's laws
W = ZQ first
W1/W2 = E1/E2 second
W = EQ/96500 merged
E = M/n-factor equiv
More formulas – J. Electrolysis
W = Z x Q ; W = E x Q/96500
NEET Snapshot:
  • Mass proportional to charge and equivalent mass.
  • 96500 C = 1 Faraday = 1 mole of electrons.
Back to Index

Deeper. Z = E/96500 g/C: silver's 0.001118 g/C is the historical standard. Multiply by current and time for deposited mass.

Chapter 7 · J. Electrolysis

Quantitative electrolysis

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12J. ElectrolysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Moles of electrons decide moles of product. Faraday: product mass follows charge passed. First law (W proportional to Q) times equivalent mass gives W = E x Q/96500. Convert grams to moles of electrons via n-factor, multiply by 96500 for coulombs, divide by current for seconds. 96500 C = 1 Faraday = 1 mole of electrons anchors everything.
Deeper. Route: grams to moles of metal, times n-factor to moles of electrons, times 96500 to coulombs. Al3+ needs 3 F per mole of Al.

Q = I x t charge
mol e- = Q/96500 convert
Al3+: 3F/mol demo
Cu2+: 2F/mol demo
t = Q/I time
More formulas – J. Electrolysis
W = Z x Q ; W = E x Q/96500

Chapter 7 · K. Batteries

Primary cells

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12K. BatteriesStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Use once: dry cell, mercury cell. Primary thrown away, secondary recharged, fuel cells fed continuously. Dry cells (1.5 V) and mercury cells (1.35 V) are single-use; lead storage (2 V per cell, six in cars) and Ni-Cd recharge by reversing discharge; H2-O2 fuel cells convert fuel continuously with water as the only product.
Deeper. Reactions are practically irreversible: Leclanche dry cell 1.5 V, mercury cell steady 1.35 V for devices.
Key Formulas – Primary cells
Zn/NH4Cl dry
HgO/Zn mercury
NEET Snapshot:
  • Use once: dry cell, mercury cell.
  • Lead storage: Pb + PbO2 + H2SO4; H2-O2 fuel cell gives water.

Chapter 7 · L. Corrosion

Electrochemical corrosion

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12L. CorrosionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Anode-cathode spots on metal surface. Iron rusts as a tiny galvanic cell; coating or cathodic protection stops it. Anodic spots oxidize iron while nearby cathodic spots reduce oxygen, needing both air and water. Barriers (paint, oil), sacrificial zinc (galvanizing) and alloying (stainless steel) each break one requirement.
Deeper. Fe oxidizes to Fe2+ at anodes while O2 + H2O reduce at cathodes; hydrated ferric oxide (rust, Fe2O3.xH2O) flakes off exposing fresh metal.
Key Formulas – Electrochemical corrosion
rust = Fe2O3.xH2O product
air + water needs
NEET Snapshot:
  • Anode-cathode spots on metal surface.
  • Rusting needs both air and water; galvanizing sacrifices zinc.

Chapter 7 · L. Corrosion

Prevention of corrosion

Redox Reactions and ElectrochemistryClass 12L. CorrosionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Paint, oil, galvanizing, alloying. Iron rusts as a tiny galvanic cell; coating or cathodic protection stops it. Anodic spots oxidize iron while nearby cathodic spots reduce oxygen, needing both air and water. Barriers (paint, oil), sacrificial zinc (galvanizing) and alloying (stainless steel) each break one requirement.
Deeper. Barrier coats block access; sacrificial zinc corrodes first (cathodic protection); chromium alloying builds passive films. Rust needs both air and water, remove either.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Paint, oil, galvanizing, alloying.
  • Rusting needs both air and water; galvanizing sacrifices zinc.

NEET Practice Set
1

Standard Zn-Cu cell EMF is (E0 Cu = +0.34 V, Zn = -0.76 V):

Solution: Ecell = 0.34 - (-0.76) = 1.10 V.
2

Nernst equation at 298 K is:

Solution: Voltage falls as the reaction quotient rises.
3

For a spontaneous cell, delta G is:

Solution: Delta G = -nFEcell; positive E gives negative G.
4

Charge to deposit 5.4 g Ag (M = 108) from Ag+ is:

Solution: 0.05 mol electrons x 96500 = 4825 C.

Index / Class 12
Chapter 8 · Step-by-step

Chemical Kinetics

Rate, order, half-life, Arrhenius, the most logic-driven Class 12 chapter. First-order half-life is constant, and that single fingerprint answers many questions outright. Always separate order (measured by experiment, can be zero or fractional) from molecularity (theoretical collision count, whole numbers only). Rate laws come from data, integrated equations give k and half-lives, and Arrhenius links k to temperature through activation energy.

~2 NEET Qs30 step-by-step topicsClass 12

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • A. Rate of reaction: rate = -delta[R]/delta t
  • D. Integrated rate equations: k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) ; t1/2 = 0.693/k
  • G. Arrhenius equation: k = A e^(-Ea/RT) ; log(k2/k1) = Ea/2.303R (1/T1 - 1/T2)
A. Rate of reactionB. Factors affecting rateC. Order of reactionD. Integrated rate equationsE. MolecularityF. Collision theoryG. Arrhenius equation

Chapter 8 · A. Rate of reaction

Chemical reaction rate

Chemical KineticsClass 12A. Rate of reactionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Concentration change per unit time. Speed of concentration change; average over interval, instantaneous at a moment. Negative sign for reactants and positive for products keep every rate positive. The rate law Rate = k[A]^m[B]^n generalizes observations, with powers from experiment and k as the unit-concentration rate.
Deeper. Negative sign for reactants (falling) and positive for products (rising) keep the number positive. Divide by stoichiometric coefficients when comparing species rates.
Key Formulas – Chemical reaction rate
rate = -d[R]/dt reactants
rate = +d[P]/dt products
mol/L/s units
More formulas – A. Rate of reaction
rate = -delta[R]/delta t
NEET Snapshot:
  • Concentration change per unit time.
  • Rate is always positive by sign convention.
Back to Index

Chapter 8 · B. Factors affecting rate

Concentration

Chemical KineticsClass 12B. Factors affecting rateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

More particles, more collisions. Concentration, temperature, catalyst, surface area and nature of reactants. More crowding means more collisions; heat pushes more molecules over Ea (roughly doubling per 10 K); catalysts open low-barrier paths unconsumed; powders expose more surface than lumps. Ionic reactions flash while covalent ones crawl.
Deeper. Rate rises with reactant crowding per the rate law powers. Zero-order reactions are the lone exception.
Key Formulas – Concentration
c up -> rate up trend
pressure = c (gas) link
NEET Snapshot:
  • More particles, more collisions.
  • Catalyst lowers activation energy without being consumed.

Index / Chemical Kinetics
Chapter 8 · B. Factors affecting rate

Temperature

Chemical KineticsClass 12B. Factors affecting rateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Roughly doubles rate per 10 K rise. Concentration, temperature, catalyst, surface area and nature of reactants. More crowding means more collisions; heat pushes more molecules over Ea (roughly doubling per 10 K); catalysts open low-barrier paths unconsumed; powders expose more surface than lumps. Ionic reactions flash while covalent ones crawl.
Deeper. Heat exponentially grows the fraction crossing Ea (Maxwell-Boltzmann tail). The thumb rule fails for precise work but guides estimates.
Key Formulas – Temperature
~2x per 10 K thumb rule
NEET Snapshot:
  • Roughly doubles rate per 10 K rise.
  • Catalyst lowers activation energy without being consumed.

Catalyst

Chemical KineticsClass 12B. Factors affecting rateStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

New low-energy path. Concentration, temperature, catalyst, surface area and nature of reactants. More crowding means more collisions; heat pushes more molecules over Ea (roughly doubling per 10 K); catalysts open low-barrier paths unconsumed; powders expose more surface than lumps. Ionic reactions flash while covalent ones crawl.
Deeper. Unchanged in mass at the end; lowers Ea for both directions equally, so equilibrium is untouched while k rises.

More crowding means more collisions; heat pushes more molecules over Ea (roughly doubling per 10 K); catalysts open low-barrier paths unconsumed; powders expose more surface than lumps. Chapter 8 · C. Order of reaction

Zero-order reaction

Chemical KineticsClass 12C. Order of reactionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Rate independent of concentration. Sum of powers in rate law, found by experiment, can be zero or fractional. Never read order from the balanced equation: complex reactions hide it in the slow step. Initial-rate experiments (double one reactant, watch the multiple) reveal each power cleanly.
Deeper. Flat rate-vs-concentration line; photochemical and surface-saturated reactions show it. Units mol/L/s.
Key Formulas – Zero-order reaction
rate = k flat
units mol/L/s units
t1/2 prop [R]0 half
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rate independent of concentration.
  • Order from rate law, not from balanced equation.
Back to Index

Chapter 8 · C. Order of reaction

First-order reaction

Chemical KineticsClass 12C. Order of reactionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Rate proportional to one concentration. Sum of powers in rate law, found by experiment, can be zero or fractional. Never read order from the balanced equation: complex reactions hide it in the slow step. Initial-rate experiments (double one reactant, watch the multiple) reveal each power cleanly.
Deeper. N2O5 decay is the classic; half-life stays constant. Units 1/s (per second).
Key Formulas – First-order reaction
rate = k[A] linear
units 1/s units
t1/2 const mark
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rate proportional to one concentration.
  • Order from rate law, not from balanced equation.

Chapter 8 · C. Order of reaction

Second-order reaction

Chemical KineticsClass 12C. Order of reactionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Proportional to squares or two reactants. Sum of powers in rate law, found by experiment, can be zero or fractional. Never read order from the balanced equation: complex reactions hide it in the slow step. Initial-rate experiments (double one reactant, watch the multiple) reveal each power cleanly.
Deeper. HI decomposition and alkaline ester hydrolysis are classics. Units L/mol/s.
Key Formulas – Second-order reaction
rate = k[A]^2 square
units L/mol/s units
t1/2 prop 1/[R]0 half
ester + OH- demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Proportional to squares or two reactants.
  • Order from rate law, not from balanced equation.

Chapter 8 · C. Order of reaction

Order determination

Chemical KineticsClass 12C. Order of reactionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Initial-rate or integrated plots. Sum of powers in rate law, found by experiment, can be zero or fractional. Never read order from the balanced equation: complex reactions hide it in the slow step. Initial-rate experiments (double one reactant, watch the multiple) reveal each power cleanly.
Deeper. Double one reactant holding others fixed: rate doubling means first power, quadrupling second, unchanged zero. Graphical half-life tests confirm.
Key Formulas – Order determination
x2 c -> x2 rate: 1st read
log plots confirm
NEET Snapshot:
  • Initial-rate or integrated plots.
  • Order from rate law, not from balanced equation.

Chapter 8 · D. Integrated rate equations

Zero-order equation

Chemical KineticsClass 12D. Integrated rate equationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

[R] = [R]0 - kt. First order: log form; half-life constant only for first order. k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) extracts k from concentration-time data; t1/2 = 0.693/k is concentration-free only here. Zero-order half-life scales with initial concentration, second-order inversely with it.
Deeper. Linear fall with time; half-life t1/2 = [R]0/2k depends on start. Completion time is finite ([R]0/k).
Key Formulas – Zero-order equation
[R] = [R]0 - kt linear
t1/2 = [R]0/2k half
units mol/L/s k
More formulas – D. Integrated rate equations
k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) ; t1/2 = 0.693/k
NEET Snapshot:
  • [R] = [R]0 - kt.
  • 75 percent completion takes two half-lives.
Back to Index

Chapter 8 · D. Integrated rate equations

Half-life

Chemical KineticsClass 12D. Integrated rate equationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Time for half to react. First order: log form; half-life constant only for first order. k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) extracts k from concentration-time data; t1/2 = 0.693/k is concentration-free only here. Zero-order half-life scales with initial concentration, second-order inversely with it.
Deeper. Constant only for first order (0.693/k). After n half-lives, 1/2^n remains: 75% done needs two, 87.5% three.
Key Formulas – Half-life
t1/2 = 0.693/k 1st order
t(3/4) = 2 x t1/2 double
1/2^n left remains
99.9%: ~10 halves
const = 1st only mark
More formulas – D. Integrated rate equations
k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) ; t1/2 = 0.693/k
NEET Snapshot:
  • Time for half to react.
  • 75 percent completion takes two half-lives.

Chapter 8 · D. Integrated rate equations

Relationship between half-life and order

Chemical KineticsClass 12D. Integrated rate equationsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Constant only for first order. First order: log form; half-life constant only for first order. k = 2.303/t log([R]0/[R]) extracts k from concentration-time data; t1/2 = 0.693/k is concentration-free only here. Zero-order half-life scales with initial concentration, second-order inversely with it.
Deeper. Zero: proportional to initial concentration; second: inversely proportional. This dependence identifies order from data tables.
Key Formulas – Relationship between half-life and order
zero: t1/2 prop [R]0 trend
second: t1/2 prop 1/[R]0 trend
const: 1st mark

  • Constant only for first order.

Chapter 8 · E. Molecularity

Molecularity

Chemical KineticsClass 12E. MolecularityStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Count in the elementary step. Molecules colliding in one elementary step; whole numbers only. Unimolecular, bimolecular and termolecular cover reality; higher simultaneous collisions are statistically impossible. Complex reactions proceed through elementary steps, and the slowest step's molecularity shapes the rate law.
Deeper. Only 1, 2 or 3 in practice; never zero or fractional. Counts colliding species, not atoms.
Key Formulas – Molecularity
1/2/3 only whole
never 0/fraction rule
4+: impossible stats
uni/bi/ter names
NEET Snapshot:
  • Count in the elementary step.
  • Order is experimental, molecularity is theoretical; complex reactions differ.
Back to Index

Topic in Depth

Single-step events. Molecules colliding in one elementary step; whole numbers only. Unimolecular, bimolecular and termolecular cover reality; higher simultaneous collisions are statistically impossible. Complex reactions proceed through elementary steps, and the slowest step's molecularity shapes the rate law.
Deeper. Order equals molecularity here and only here. The slow elementary step (RDS) governs complex rates.
Key Formulas – Elementary reactions
order = molecularity single step
propose + verify method
NEET Snapshot:
  • Single-step events.
  • Order is experimental, molecularity is theoretical; complex reactions differ.

Chapter 8 · E. Molecularity

Difference between order and molecularity

Chemical KineticsClass 12E. MolecularityStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Order can be zero/fractional, molecularity never. Molecules colliding in one elementary step; whole numbers only. Unimolecular, bimolecular and termolecular cover reality; higher simultaneous collisions are statistically impossible. Complex reactions proceed through elementary steps, and the slowest step's molecularity shapes the rate law.
Deeper. Order is measured, molecularity is theorized; H2 + I2 is second order and bimolecular, but H2 + Br2 is fractional order with no single molecularity.
Key Formulas – Difference between order and molecularity
H2+I2: 2/bimol match
H2+Br2: fractional mismatch
NEET Snapshot:
  • Order can be zero/fractional, molecularity never.
  • Order is experimental, molecularity is theoretical; complex reactions differ.

Index / Chemical Kinetics
Chapter 8 · F. Collision theory

Collision theory

Chemical KineticsClass 12F. Collision theoryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Rate from collision frequency and fraction effective. Only energetic, properly oriented hits react. Rate equals collision frequency times steric factor times Boltzmann fraction: k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT). Energy threshold plus geometry decide success; the vast majority of collisions bounce off uselessly.
Deeper. k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT): Z counts hits, the exponential counts energetic ones, P counts well-aimed ones. Explains temperature and concentration effects together.
Key Formulas – Collision theory
k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT) master
explains T + c power
NEET Snapshot:
  • Rate from collision frequency and fraction effective.
  • Steric factor plus energy threshold decide success.
Back to Index
Index / Chemical Kinetics
Chapter 8 · F. Collision theory

Effective collisions

Chemical KineticsClass 12F. Collision theoryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Right energy plus right orientation. Only energetic, properly oriented hits react. Rate equals collision frequency times steric factor times Boltzmann fraction: k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT). Energy threshold plus geometry decide success; the vast majority of collisions bounce off uselessly.
Deeper. A tiny fraction of total hits qualifies: energy above Ea AND correct geometry. Complex molecules need stricter orientation (small P).
Key Formulas – Effective collisions
E >= Ea + geometry both
NEET Snapshot:
  • Right energy plus right orientation.
  • Steric factor plus energy threshold decide success.

Chapter 8 · F. Collision theory

Activation energy

Chemical KineticsClass 12F. Collision theoryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Minimum extra energy to react. Only energetic, properly oriented hits react. Rate equals collision frequency times steric factor times Boltzmann fraction: k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT). Energy threshold plus geometry decide success; the vast majority of collisions bounce off uselessly.
Deeper. The barrier between reactants and products via the transition state; equals threshold minus average energy. Catalysts lower it.
Key Formulas – Activation energy
Ea = threshold - average gap
NEET Snapshot:
  • Minimum extra energy to react.
  • Steric factor plus energy threshold decide success.

Index / Chemical Kinetics
Chapter 8 · F. Collision theory

Threshold energy

Chemical KineticsClass 12F. Collision theoryStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Floor energy for collision success. Only energetic, properly oriented hits react. Rate equals collision frequency times steric factor times Boltzmann fraction: k = PZ e^(-Ea/RT). Energy threshold plus geometry decide success; the vast majority of collisions bounce off uselessly.
Deeper. Below it every collision bounces off elastically. The Maxwell-Boltzmann tail above it grows fast with temperature.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Floor energy for collision success.
  • Steric factor plus energy threshold decide success.

Index / Chemical Kinetics
Chapter 8 · G. Arrhenius equation

Arrhenius equation

Chemical KineticsClass 12G. Arrhenius equationStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

k from A, Ea and T. k explodes with temperature via activation barrier. k = A e^(-Ea/RT) separates collision frequency (A) from energy success (exponential). Log form compares two temperatures, and the log k vs 1/T slope (-Ea/2.303R) measures barriers graphically.
Deeper. Exponential temperature dependence explains the doubling-per-10K rule. A is nearly temperature-independent.
Key Formulas – Arrhenius equation
k = A e^(-Ea/RT) master
More formulas – G. Arrhenius equation
k = A e^(-Ea/RT) ; log(k2/k1) = Ea/2.303R (1/T1 - 1/T2)
NEET Snapshot:
  • k from A, Ea and T.
  • Plot log k vs 1/T: slope = -Ea/2.303R.
Back to Index

Chemical Kinetics: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

For k = 0.0693 min-1 (first order), half-life is:

Solution: t1/2 = 0.693/k = 10 min.
2

Order of a reaction is determined by:

Solution: Order comes from rate data, never from the equation alone.
3

A catalyst increases rate by:

Solution: New low-barrier path; equilibrium untouched.
4

Units of a first-order rate constant are:

Solution: k = rate/concentration gives per-second.

Chapter 9 · Step-by-step

Surface Chemistry

Adsorption vs absorption, Freundlich, colloids and emulsions, the most definition-driven Class 12 chapter. No heavy math: one graph (Freundlich log-log line) plus one-line differences carry every mark. Master four contrasts (adsorption/absorption, physisorption/chemisorption, lyophilic/lyophobic, o/w vs w/o emulsions) and four colloid properties (Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation), and the chapter is done.

~1 NEET Q30 step-by-step topicsClass 12

Chapter Formula Sheet

Must-memorise formulas
  • C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm: x/m = kP^(1/n) ; log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P

Index / Surface Chemistry
Chapter 9 · A. Adsorption

Adsorption

Surface ChemistryClass 12A. AdsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Species collect on a surface. Sticking ON the surface (adsorption) vs soaking IN (absorption). Physisorption is weak van der Waals multilayering (20-40 kJ/mol, reversible, falls with heat); chemisorption is true chemical bonding in one monolayer (80-240 kJ/mol, needs activation energy). Heat values alone classify them in questions.
Deeper. Charcoal trapping poisonous gases in masks is the classic; silica gel guarding medicines is another. Surface phenomenon only.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Species collect on a surface.
  • Physisorption is weak and reversible; chemisorption strong and specific.
Back to Index
Index / Surface Chemistry
Chapter 9 · A. Adsorption

Absorption

Surface ChemistryClass 12A. AdsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Species enter the bulk. Sticking ON the surface (adsorption) vs soaking IN (absorption). Physisorption is weak van der Waals multilayering (20-40 kJ/mol, reversible, falls with heat); chemisorption is true chemical bonding in one monolayer (80-240 kJ/mol, needs activation energy). Heat values alone classify them in questions.
Deeper. Sponge soaking water and cotton absorbing ink spread throughout the volume. Bulk phenomenon throughout.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Species enter the bulk.
  • Physisorption is weak and reversible; chemisorption strong and specific.

Chapter 9 · A. Adsorption

Difference between adsorption and absorption

Surface ChemistryClass 12A. AdsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Surface vs bulk. Sticking ON the surface (adsorption) vs soaking IN (absorption). Physisorption is weak van der Waals multilayering (20-40 kJ/mol, reversible, falls with heat); chemisorption is true chemical bonding in one monolayer (80-240 kJ/mol, needs activation energy). Heat values alone classify them in questions.
Deeper. Sorption covers both together; desorption reverses adsorption. Exam one-liners always contrast ON versus IN.

sorption = both term

  • Physisorption is weak and reversible; chemisorption strong and specific.

Chapter 9 · A. Adsorption

Physisorption

Surface ChemistryClass 12A. AdsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Van der Waals forces, low heat, multilayer. Sticking ON the surface (adsorption) vs soaking IN (absorption). Physisorption is weak van der Waals multilayering (20-40 kJ/mol, reversible, falls with heat); chemisorption is true chemical bonding in one monolayer (80-240 kJ/mol, needs activation energy). Heat values alone classify them in questions.
Deeper. Reversible and weakened by heating; no activation energy needed. Noble gases on charcoal show it purely.
Key Formulas – Physisorption
20-40 kJ/mol low heat

  • Physisorption is weak and reversible; chemisorption strong and specific.

Chapter 9 · A. Adsorption

Chemisorption

Surface ChemistryClass 12A. AdsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Chemical bonds, high heat, monolayer. Sticking ON the surface (adsorption) vs soaking IN (absorption). Physisorption is weak van der Waals multilayering (20-40 kJ/mol, reversible, falls with heat); chemisorption is true chemical bonding in one monolayer (80-240 kJ/mol, needs activation energy). Heat values alone classify them in questions.
Deeper. Irreversible and specific like H2 on nickel; needs activation energy and stops at one layer. Catalysis begins here.
Key Formulas – Chemisorption
80-240 kJ/mol high heat
NEET Snapshot:
  • Chemical bonds, high heat, monolayer.
  • Physisorption is weak and reversible; chemisorption strong and specific.

Chapter 9 · B. Factors affecting adsorption

Surface area

Surface ChemistryClass 12B. Factors affecting adsorptionStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Finer particles adsorb more. More area, right temperature and pressure, right pair of materials. Finer particles multiply surface; low temperature keeps weak layers stuck; high pressure pushes more gas on; porous adsorbents and easily liquefiable gases pair best. Each factor is one crisp trend line.
Deeper. Powdered charcoal beats lumps gram for gram; activated carbon maximizes pores deliberately. Catalysts are powdered for the same reason.
Key Formulas – Surface area
area up -> up trend
NEET Snapshot:
  • Finer particles adsorb more.
  • Gases adsorb more when easily liquefiable; physisorption falls with rising T.

Finer particles multiply surface; low temperature keeps weak layers stuck; high pressure pushes more gas on; porous adsorbents and easily liquefiable gases pair best. Each factor is one crisp trend line.
Deeper. Heat shakes weakly held layers off; chemisorption first rises (needs Ea) then falls. Low-temperature preference marks physisorption.

  • Low T favours physisorption.
  • Gases adsorb more when easily liquefiable; physisorption falls with rising T.

Chapter 9 · C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm

Freundlich equation

Surface ChemistryClass 12C. Freundlich adsorption isothermStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Power-law link of amount and pressure. x/m rises with pressure but with flattening power law. The empirical x/m = kP^(1/n) straightens into log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P with slope 1/n between 0 and 1. It fails at very high pressure where real surfaces saturate but the power law keeps climbing.
Deeper. Empirical and valid at moderate pressures; k measures capacity, n measures intensity. x/m is mass adsorbed per gram adsorbent.
Key Formulas – Freundlich equation
x/m = kP^(1/n) master
k = capacity read
n = intensity read
x/m units g/g
More formulas – C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm
x/m = kP^(1/n) ; log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P
NEET Snapshot:
  • Power-law link of amount and pressure.
  • Slope of log-log plot is 1/n between 0 and 1.

Chapter 9 · C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm

Graphical representation

Surface ChemistryClass 12C. Freundlich adsorption isothermStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Straight log-log line. x/m rises with pressure but with flattening power law. The empirical x/m = kP^(1/n) straightens into log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P with slope 1/n between 0 and 1. It fails at very high pressure where real surfaces saturate but the power law keeps climbing.
Deeper. Intercept gives log k, slope gives 1/n. Curved raw plots versus straight log plots is a standard identify-the-graph question.
Key Formulas – Graphical representation
log(x/m) vs log P straight
slope = 1/n read
intercept = log k read
0<1/n<1 range
More formulas – C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm
x/m = kP^(1/n) ; log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P
NEET Snapshot:

  • Slope of log-log plot is 1/n between 0 and 1.

Chapter 9 · C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm

Limitations

Surface ChemistryClass 12C. Freundlich adsorption isothermStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Fails at very high pressure saturation. x/m rises with pressure but with flattening power law. The empirical x/m = kP^(1/n) straightens into log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P with slope 1/n between 0 and 1. It fails at very high pressure where real surfaces saturate but the power law keeps climbing.
Deeper. The model keeps rising where real monolayers flatten; 1/n must stay between 0 and 1. Langmuir fixes saturation with its own isotherm.
Key Formulas – Limitations
0 < 1/n < 1 range
More formulas – C. Freundlich adsorption isotherm
x/m = kP^(1/n) ; log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P
NEET Snapshot:
  • Fails at very high pressure saturation.
  • Slope of log-log plot is 1/n between 0 and 1.

Chapter 9 · D. Catalysis

Catalyst

Surface ChemistryClass 12D. CatalysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Speeds up without being consumed. Catalysts open cheap paths; enzymes are biological specialists. Homogeneous catalysts share the reactants' phase, heterogeneous ones work on surfaces, enzymes use lock-and-key specificity near body conditions. All stay unconsumed and none shift equilibrium.
Deeper. Tiny amounts deliver huge rate gains with full specificity; poisons and promoters tune them. Unchanged mass at the end is definitional.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Speeds up without being consumed.
  • Homogeneous: same phase; heterogeneous: different phase.
Back to Index
Index / Surface Chemistry

Homogeneous catalysis

Surface ChemistryClass 12D. CatalysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Same phase as reactants. Catalysts open cheap paths; enzymes are biological specialists. Homogeneous catalysts share the reactants' phase, heterogeneous ones work on surfaces, enzymes use lock-and-key specificity near body conditions. All stay unconsumed and none shift equilibrium.
Deeper. NO in the lead-chamber sulphuric process; acid catalysis of esterification. No surface step involved.

gas/gas, liquid phases

Chapter 9 · D. Catalysis

Heterogeneous catalysis

Surface ChemistryClass 12D. CatalysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Surface-based, different phase. Catalysts open cheap paths; enzymes are biological specialists. Homogeneous catalysts share the reactants' phase, heterogeneous ones work on surfaces, enzymes use lock-and-key specificity near body conditions. All stay unconsumed and none shift equilibrium.
Deeper. Haber's iron bed and contact-process V2O5 work by adsorption-reaction-desorption cycles. Surface area rules effectiveness.
NEET Snapshot:

  • Homogeneous: same phase; heterogeneous: different phase.

Chapter 9 · D. Catalysis

Enzyme catalysis

Surface ChemistryClass 12D. CatalysisStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Protein catalysts, lock-and-key. Catalysts open cheap paths; enzymes are biological specialists. Homogeneous catalysts share the reactants' phase, heterogeneous ones work on surfaces, enzymes use lock-and-key specificity near body conditions. All stay unconsumed and none shift equilibrium.
Deeper. Pepsin, invertase and zymase work near body temperature and pH with extreme specificity. Denaturation by heat or pH kills them.

10^8x faster power

Chapter 9 · E. Colloids

Colloidal solution

Surface ChemistryClass 12E. ColloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Heterogeneous yet stable-looking mix. In-between particles (1-1000 nm) that scatter light but pass filters. True solutions (<1 nm) stay invisible, suspensions (>1000 nm) settle, colloids scatter (Tyndall) yet look stable. Lyophilic sols love solvent and self-stabilize; lyophobic sols hate it and need added stabilizers.
Deeper. Milk, fog, blood and smoke are daily examples; particles span 1-1000 nm. Pass filter paper, blocked by parchment.
Key Formulas – Colloidal solution
1-1000 nm size
NEET Snapshot:
  • Heterogeneous yet stable-looking mix.
  • Lyophilic love solvent and are stable; lyophobic need stabilizers.
Back to Index
Index / Surface Chemistry

Classification of colloids

Surface ChemistryClass 12E. ColloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

By phase pairs and solvent love. In-between particles (1-1000 nm) that scatter light but pass filters. True solutions (<1 nm) stay invisible, suspensions (>1000 nm) settle, colloids scatter (Tyndall) yet look stable. Lyophilic sols love solvent and self-stabilize; lyophobic sols hate it and need added stabilizers.
Deeper. Eight physical types (sol, gel, aerosol, emulsion, foam...) plus the lyophilic/lyophobic stability split. Examples per type are asked directly.

sol/gel states
aerosol/foam states

Chapter 9 · F. Properties of colloids

Tyndall effect

Surface ChemistryClass 12F. Properties of colloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Light beam becomes visible. Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation: four names, four one-liners. Light scattering proves particles, zigzag motion proves molecular hits, field migration proves charge, electrolyte settling proves neutralizability. Hardy-Schulze ranks coagulating power by opposite-ion charge.
Deeper. Headlight cones in fog and projector beams in dusty halls show scattering by particles. True solutions stay dark.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Light beam becomes visible.
  • Hardy-Schulze: higher opposite charge coagulates faster.
Back to Index
Index / Surface Chemistry
Chapter 9 · F. Properties of colloids

Brownian movement

Surface ChemistryClass 12F. Properties of colloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Zigzag from molecular hits. Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation: four names, four one-liners. Light scattering proves particles, zigzag motion proves molecular hits, field migration proves charge, electrolyte settling proves neutralizability. Hardy-Schulze ranks coagulating power by opposite-ion charge.
Deeper. Unequal bombardment by medium molecules jiggles particles; Einstein's analysis proved atoms real. Opposes settling, stabilizing sols.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Zigzag from molecular hits.
  • Hardy-Schulze: higher opposite charge coagulates faster.
Back to Index
Index / Surface Chemistry
Chapter 9 · F. Properties of colloids

Electrophoresis

Surface ChemistryClass 12F. Properties of colloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Migration in electric field. Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation: four names, four one-liners. Light scattering proves particles, zigzag motion proves molecular hits, field migration proves charge, electrolyte settling proves neutralizability. Hardy-Schulze ranks coagulating power by opposite-ion charge.
Deeper. Movement direction reveals particle charge sign; used to separate proteins. Like charges migrating together prove uniform charge.
NEET Snapshot:
  • Migration in electric field.
  • Hardy-Schulze: higher opposite charge coagulates faster.

Chapter 9 · F. Properties of colloids

Coagulation

Surface ChemistryClass 12F. Properties of colloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Settling on adding electrolytes. Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation: four names, four one-liners. Light scattering proves particles, zigzag motion proves molecular hits, field migration proves charge, electrolyte settling proves neutralizability. Hardy-Schulze ranks coagulating power by opposite-ion charge.
Deeper. Opposite ions neutralize stabilizing charge and particles clump. Hardy-Schulze: Al3+ beats Mg2+ beats Na+ for negative sols.
Key Formulas – Coagulation
Al3+>Mg2+>Na+ order
NEET Snapshot:
  • Settling on adding electrolytes.
  • Hardy-Schulze: higher opposite charge coagulates faster.

Chapter 9 · F. Properties of colloids

Charge on colloidal particles

Surface ChemistryClass 12F. Properties of colloidsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Decides stability and migration. Tyndall, Brownian, electrophoresis, coagulation: four names, four one-liners. Light scattering proves particles, zigzag motion proves molecular hits, field migration proves charge, electrolyte settling proves neutralizability. Hardy-Schulze ranks coagulating power by opposite-ion charge.
Deeper. Like charges repel and keep sols stable; neutralization collapses them. Hydrated ferric oxide sol is positive, gold sol negative.
Key Formulas – Charge on colloidal particles
like = stable rule
Fe2O3 sol +ve example
neutralize = fall logic
NEET Snapshot:
  • Decides stability and migration.
  • Hardy-Schulze: higher opposite charge coagulates faster.

Index / Surface Chemistry
Chapter 9 · G. Emulsions

Emulsion

Surface ChemistryClass 12G. EmulsionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Two immiscible liquids dispersed. Liquid-in-liquid colloids held by emulsifiers. Oil-in-water (milk) versus water-in-oil (butter) is the core split, tested by dilution or dye-solubility. Soaps and proteins coat droplets against merging; without them the liquids separate.
Deeper. Needs shaking plus stabilizer; otherwise phases separate on standing. Daily life runs on emulsions.
Key Formulas – Emulsion
shake + stabilize make
NEET Snapshot:
  • Two immiscible liquids dispersed.
  • Milk is oil-in-water; butter is water-in-oil.
Back to Index

Chapter 9 · G. Emulsions

Types of emulsions

Surface ChemistryClass 12G. EmulsionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Oil-in-water vs water-in-oil. Liquid-in-liquid colloids held by emulsifiers. Oil-in-water (milk) versus water-in-oil (butter) is the core split, tested by dilution or dye-solubility. Soaps and proteins coat droplets against merging; without them the liquids separate.
Deeper. Dilution test (mixes with water = o/w) and dye-solubility identify them. Milk is o/w, butter and cold cream w/o.
Key Formulas – Types of emulsions
o/w: milk example
w/o: butter example
cold cream w/o demo
NEET Snapshot:
  • Oil-in-water vs water-in-oil.
  • Milk is oil-in-water; butter is water-in-oil.

Chapter 9 · G. Emulsions

Emulsifying agents

Surface ChemistryClass 12G. EmulsionsStep-by-step Topic

Topic in Depth

Soaps and proteins stabilize them. Liquid-in-liquid colloids held by emulsifiers. Oil-in-water (milk) versus water-in-oil (butter) is the core split, tested by dilution or dye-solubility. Soaps and proteins coat droplets against merging; without them the liquids separate.
Deeper. They coat droplets with protective films blocking coalescence. Gum arabic and gelatin are food-grade examples.
Key Formulas – Emulsifying agents
soap/gum/protein examples
NEET Snapshot:
  • Soaps and proteins stabilize them.
  • Milk is oil-in-water; butter is water-in-oil.

Chapter 9 · Practice

Surface Chemistry: NEET Practice Set

4 MCQsWith solutions
NEET Practice Set
1

Tyndall effect is shown by:

Solution: Colloidal particles scatter a light beam.
2

In Freundlich isotherm, the slope of the log-log plot is:

Solution: log(x/m) = log k + (1/n) log P.
3

Which is true of chemisorption?

Solution: Chemical bonds form a single layer.
4

Milk is an example of:

Solution: Fat droplets dispersed in water.
